# Findings — current knowledge

⭐ **This file holds no history.** It is rewritten freely whenever understanding changes, and
it states only what is believed **now**. What was believed before, and when, is `LOG.md`.
If a claim is not in the ledger below, it is not established.

---

## ⛔⛔ PLACEMENT: READ THIS BEFORE TRUSTING ANY NUMBER BELOW

**Every measurement in this project up to 2026-09-11 was taken with the tag on the BACK of
the Chameleon Ultra. The reading side is the FRONT, where the buttons are. It is worth
21x.**

That is not a caveat, it is the dominant term. The whole investigation ran at roughly 1/20
of the available signal — about 26 dB — which is most of the "31.2 dB below the Proxmark"
the project spent days trying to explain. The PSK1/PSK2 decoder bug was one cause of that
figure; the tag being on the wrong side of the device was the other, and larger, one.

⚠ The Flipper Zero reads from its back, which is where the assumption came from. The
Chameleon is the other way round.

| | tag on the BACK | tag on the FRONT |
|---|---|---|
| fc/2 skirt over the empty floor | 1.0–3.7x | **20–34x** |
| single-capture decode rate | 51/160 = 32% | **34/48 = 71%** |
| sample phase 0 (the stock trigger) | 0/5 | **3/3** |
| sample phases 96–120 | 0/5 | **3/3** |
| the weak "white" coil | 0/12, never read | **reads first try** |

⇒ Claims measured on the back are marked **⚠B**. They are not automatically wrong — many
are relative comparisons that survive a common scale factor — but none of them has been
confirmed at the signal level the device actually delivers, and several are now known to be
artefacts. `NEXT.md` §1 is the re-measurement.

---

## The result

**The Chameleon Ultra reads Indala, on the device, as a command** — with the tag on the
**front**, 71% of single captures decode and every sample phase outside 56–88 works,
including the stock phase 0.

```
lf indala read   ->   Indala PSK1
                      Raw: a0000000e6bd0e92
                      Fmt 26 FC: 52 Card: 63612 Parity: 11
```

20 of 20 consecutive reads returned the credential, in 0.41–0.55 s each. The whole
demodulation is integer arithmetic on the nRF52840 — no float, no FFT, 8 KB of buffer.

Offline, the same decoder recovers the credential from 51 of 160 single 300 ms captures,
and the empty field produced a frame **0 times in 160**. No stacking, no folding, no
averaging.

## The decode, end to end

Everything below is integer arithmetic over 4096 samples. No float, no FFT.

```
sample at 125 kHz, carrier-locked, phase in the working window
  -> mix by (-1)^n                    fc/2 is exactly fs/2, so this is the whole mixer:
                                      no oscillator, no phase estimate. DC and slow drift
                                      move UP to fs/2 and fall out.
  -> [1,2,1] notch at fs/2            NOT optional, see C03. And it is a NOTCH, not a
                                      cutoff: its job is to remove what the mix just
                                      moved up there, see C16.
  -> 32-sample boxcar per bit         the optimal filter for a rectangular bit at RF/32
  -> threshold: bit = (integrator > 0)  PSK1 — the phase IS the data, see C02
  -> search preamble64 = 1010 + 28 zeros + 1, normal and inverted
  -> read 64 bits from the preamble position
```

⚠ **Three details are each individually fatal.** Any one of them alone takes the decode
from 51/160 to 0/160 or near it: the PSK1 mapping (C02), the baseband filter (C03), and
**not** discarding the settle window (C04).

⚠ **And a fourth detail is fatal to trusting the answer rather than getting one.** One in
five recovered frames is WRONG (C17). A reader that returns the first frame it decodes
returns a wrong credential about 20% of the time, so the firmware requires two captures to
agree (C18).

## Claims ledger

Every load-bearing claim, with the evidence that supports it. **`indep` is the column this
project learned the hard way**: whether the claim was checked against something that does
not share its own code path. The retracted `+8.2 dB` had n=20 and a null — and no
independent check, which is exactly why it survived for a day.

`null` — was a control run, and what kind. `emptyfield` is the real control here; `white`
noise is a much weaker one against a chain whose floor spans 17x across bands.

### Firmware bugs found and fixed

| id | bug | effect | ref |
|---|---|---|---|
| F01 | The 8-bit debug path did `14-bit >> 5` | discarded 5 bits **and distorted the rolloff shape** | L10 |
| F02 | BLE advertising collapsed the LF field during capture | 41% of captures corrupted → 0% | L26 |
| F03 | The DMA ring dropped 75% of every batch (`CIRCULAR_BUFFER_SIZE` 512 vs `ADC_BUF_SIZE` 2048) | captures were islands of 512 samples spanning 64 ms, not 16 ms | L01 |

### Retracted

| claim | why it was wrong | retracted by |
|---|---|---|
| **"the working phase window is ticks 4–60; the stock phase 0 decodes 0/5"** (C06) | ⛔ a placement artefact. With the tag on the FRONT, phase 0 decodes 3/3 and so do phases 96–120. The surviving dead zone is 56–88, and it carries the HIGHEST skirt of the sweep — so it is a genuine polarity null, not weak signal | L57 |
| "the working margin is tiny — ~1.5x over the floor reads, ~1.1x does not" (C27) | ⛔ measured entirely on the back. On the front the same coils sit at 20–34x | L57 |
| "a second Indala coil is inaudible / 3.3x weaker" (C26, C32) | ⛔ both coils were on the back. On the front the weak one reads first try | L57 |
| "tag position is worth ~5.7 dB" | ⛔ understated by a factor of ten. Side of the device alone is 21x, ~26 dB | L57 |
| "fc/2 sits at Nyquist, therefore it cancels" | the Proxmark samples once per carrier cycle too, and reads Indala | L03 |
| "the Indala tag produces no detectable modulation" | measured through the `>>5` truncation | L10 |
| "phase sweep shows 659x" | tracking overrun bursts; small denominator | L15 |
| "31.2 dB below the Proxmark ⇒ not viable" | the **measurement** stands; the conclusion assumed a decoder that could not work | L44 |
| "7.6 dB demodulation gap, confirmed twice" | both routes measured that broken decoder | L44 |
| "detectable but not decodable, 52/64 vs a 45/64 null" | a constant preamble run scoring itself — and the wrong decoder besides | L42, L44 |
| "matched filter worth +8.2 dB over PSKDemod" | measured on a synthetic that shared the decoder's own bug | L44 |
| "the phase is gone before the ADC" | the injected synthetic shared the decoder's convention; it was testing self-consistency | L44 |
| "coherent frame averaging is dead" | the circularity argument was wrong — captures are frame-locked, so there is no alignment step | L40 |
| "folding at 2048 samples improves things" | it averages a frame against its own inverse; only the polarity-blind skirt survives | L41 |
| "settle has no effect" / "air gap is flat" | both measured pre-BLE-fix on a tag carrying `DEADBEEF/12345678` | L34 |
| "the baseband low-pass needs a ~12 kHz cutoff" | the cutoff was incidental. What the filter must do is NULL fs/2, where the mixer just put the carrier ripple; a 3-tap notch does it better and cheaper (C16) | L46 |

### Open / untested

| claim | status |
|---|---|
| Tag position is worth ~5.7 dB | n=1, from an accidental probe. Large, concentrated at high frequency, and plausible — but one capture |
| Settle, air gap, oversampling | closures invalid (L34); never re-measured against a working decoder |
| Does a second **physical** tag read? | ⛔ **NO** — C26, and it is silicon not payload: both tags carry the same word, both read on a Flipper, the thin copper coin reads on the Chameleon and the white coin does not. C28 shows its signal is present but 1.4x weaker, which on a ~1.5x margin is the whole difference |
| Why does stacking not rescue the weak coil? | ⛔ open (C34). Best current story: a weakly-coupled T5577 charges more slowly and more variably, so its frame start jitters between captures and there is nothing to add coherently. ⇒ align on the PREAMBLE the decoder already finds, rather than assuming lag 0 |
| What is the common background? | ⛔ open and it defeats every filter tried (C31, C35). Empty captures correlate at 0.92–0.95 after mixing, band-limiting to 6 kHz, removing DC and dropping 1024 samples. Not the odd/even imbalance (1.7–2.1 counts, 0.1% of the ripple). Until it is identified, no cross-capture correlation on this bench means what it appears to mean |
| Can the Chameleon WRITE a T5577 reliably? | ⛔ not known to work — one of three raw blocks landed, and the proven multi-block path changed nothing. Undiagnosable without a T5577 block read, which the device does not have. `NEXT.md` §7 |
| C38 | ⛔⛔ **THE SKIRT DOES NOT PREDICT DECODE — it is anti-correlated with it over half the sweep.** The fc/2 skirt is a smooth single-peaked curve: **highest at tick 44 (212), which decodes 5/5**, lowest at 116–120 (134), which also decodes 5/5. The dead band 60–92 sits in the MIDDLE of the skirt range (145–200). ⇒ the sweep scripts in NEXT §7 score a quantity that cannot rank what they were used to rank | 32 phases x 5, skirt and decode measured on the same captures | ✓ decode and skirt come from the identical capture set | ✓ predicted by M8 (the skirt is polarity-blind); this is the direct test | L58 |
| C39 | ⛔⛔ **IN THE DEAD BAND THE DECODER RETURNS A DETERMINISTIC WRONG FRAME, WHICH DEFEATS THE TWO-CAPTURE AGREEMENT RULE.** Phase 64 returns `a0000000b5af0b92` on **5 of 5** captures, phase 88 `a0000000c6b90c92` on **5 of 5**, phase 92 `a0000000c6b90e92` on 4 of 5. Two independent captures therefore AGREE on a wrong credential and `indala_read()` returns it. The frames are not aligned to the data, so their weakest bit integrator cancels to near zero — which is what C48's gate rejects. ⚠ Wiegand-26 parity rejects two of the three but **passes `a0000000c6b90e92`**, which reports the CORRECT facility code 52 with a wrong card number | 5 captures at each of 32 phases | ✓ the 22 good phases produce 0 wrong frames in 114 decodes | ✓ mfdemod.py independently produces wrong words at the same phases | L58 |
| C40 | ⭐⭐ **MORE SIGNAL MADE THE FAILURE MODE WORSE, NOT BETTER.** On the back (26 dB down) all 17 wrong frames were DISTINCT — errors were noise-driven, so they scattered, which is exactly what C17/C18 measured and built the acceptance rule on. On the front, 3 wrong words repeat within a phase, two of them 5/5. ⇒ **"wrong words never repeat" is a property of low SNR, not of the decoder**, and the rule it justifies does not cover the strong-signal case | 160 back + 160 front tag captures, same decoder | ✓ same tag, same decoder, same capture length; only placement differs | ✓ the two capture sets were taken weeks apart | L58 |
| C41 | ⚠ **C03 HAS REVERSED SIGN: the fs/2 notch now costs decodes.** Shipping C decoder on the front: notch ON 114 truth / 21 wrong, notch OFF (plain boxcar) **121 truth** / 26 wrong; empty stays 0/160 both ways. On the back it was 43->51 and strictly dominant. The mechanism it was justified by — ~9 counts of carrier ripple leaking into a ~10-count subcarrier — is gone at 20x the subcarrier. ⇒ no longer load-bearing; it now trades ~7 decodes for ~5 fewer wrong frames | 160 front captures, both builds | ✓ same captures, same binary except the 3-tap fold | ✓ numpy decoder reproduces the reversal (107 with lpf, 121 without) | L58 |
| C42 | ⭐ **C04 SURVIVES INTACT AND IT WAS NEVER ABOUT SNR.** Discarding a 400-sample settle window from the capture still takes the decode from 107/160 to **0/160** on the front, unchanged from the back. It is structural: 400 samples removes the first frame's preamble and leaves too few bits after the second. ⇒ the only one of the three "individually fatal" details that is fatal for a reason 26 dB cannot touch | 160 front captures, cut 0 vs cut 400 | ✓ same captures, only the cut differs | ✓ same result as the back-side measurement | L58 |
| C43 | ⚠ **Integrator amplitude separates truth from wrong with no overlap here — but it is not a gate.** Truth 7240–12699, wrong 5120–6833. The gap is real and matches the half-bit mechanism (a straddling integrator recovers ~half the energy), but amplitude scales with coupling, so a weakly-coupled tag would fall under any fixed threshold. ⇒ a diagnostic, not an acceptance test | 135 frames from 160 front captures | ✓ both classes from the same sweep | — | L58 |
| C44 | ⭐⭐ **THE LOUD-SIGNAL NULL PASSES, against a genuinely loud interferer.** An HID Prox tag measured at **88-100x the empty-field floor in its own band** produced `LF tag not found` on **30 of 30** `lf indala read` attempts. The 10-18kHz empty floor was measured for the first time here: **63322, range 56395-69012** (n=63). Tag present: mean 6307327, range 6097398-6515642 (n=26), a clean two-regime split as the tag was lifted mid-run | 30 reads + 90 continuous band measurements | ✓ the empty floor came from the SAME run, same band, same session — the tag leaving is the control | ✓ carrier DC shifts 5875 -> 5446 at the same sample, independently marking the tag's departure | L59 |
| C45 | ⛔ **`lf hid prox read` fails on a 90x signal with the RF path provably flat.** ⭐ **SUPERSEDED IN ITS HEADLINE BY C250, which found the cause: a BLE advertising burst collapsing the field mid-capture.** The observation below stands exactly as measured — the amplitude really is flat across successes and failures — but "the intermittency is the DECODER" was the wrong conclusion to draw from it. The RF path is flat *as sampled between bursts*; the burst is a 1.6 ms hole that no amplitude median taken around it can show. Stress run on the white coin: subcarrier median 6034358 idle, 6023801 under an Indala load, 6020802 recovering — **1.00x throughout** — while reads went 8/8, 7/8, 7/8. The two failures sat at the same amplitude as the successes. ⚠ And the Indala load does NOT degrade HID reads, which is M17/M18 confirmed with a continuous metric rather than a binary one | 24 reads + 24 amplitude samples, 3 phases | ✓ amplitude measured on every single read, same session | ✓ a second tag at 6.31M (within 5% of this one's 6.03M) reads 0/6 — same level, opposite outcome | L59, L60 |
| F05 | ⛔ **A FAILED READ IS NOT EVIDENCE THE TAG IS ABSENT, and NEXT §3 told us to use it that way.** The bracketing rule said to confirm the probe tag's coupling with a read immediately before and after. Followed literally here it would have thrown away a VALID null: all 6 bracketing reads failed while the tag sat at 90x the floor. ⇒ Bracket with `lfprobe.py`, which measures presence directly, not with the very binary metric whose unreliability is why the rule exists | 6 failed bracket reads against 26 amplitude samples | ✓ the amplitude and the reads come from the same minutes | ✓ this is M17/M18's lesson recurring in a new place | L59 |
| C46 | ⭐⭐ **THREE TAGS, BYTE-IDENTICAL MEMORY, THREE DIFFERENT DECODE RATES — so it is the PACKAGE, not the payload, the config or the silicon.** Blue dual, white coin and copper coin all dump page 0 as `00107060 / 1D555A6A / 5595A555 / 95A95969` and page 1 as `00107060 / E0150A4C / 5B020499` — the page-1 factory trace data is supposed to be unique per chip, so these are the same clone silicon from one lot. A Proxmark reads all three perfectly (`raw 000000000000003708c08e26`). The Chameleon reads them **0/6, 3/6 and 7/9** | 3 tags x (PM3 dump + PM3 read + 6-9 Chameleon reads) | ✓ the tags are each other's control — everything except the package is held identical | ✓ the PM3 is a second reader and it does not discriminate between them | L60 |
| C47 | ⚠ **Only `lf_reader_generic.c` suspends BLE advertising, and the readers that duplicate its capture path instead of sharing it are the ones that fail.** The LF readers split into two families: **GPIO/comparator** (em410x, jablotron, viking — 128-entry rings, `register_rio_callback`, no SAADC at all) and **SAADC** (hidprox, ioprox, pac, plus lf_reader_generic). Each SAADC reader rolls its own `saadc_cb` + ring + field start/stop; only `lf_reader_generic.c` also suspends advertising, and its comment records why — a burst collapses the 125kHz field for ~1.6ms and hit **4 captures in 10**. ⇒ Of the three SAADC readers without the guard, **two are the ones measured failing on loud tags** (HID 0/6 to 22/24, PAC 0/5 and 2/5); ioProx is untested. The SAADC reader that HAS the guard is Indala, at 60/60. ⛔ I previously weakened this on `lf em 410x read` scoring 95% — **that was a bad comparison**: EM410x is on the GPIO path and never touches the SAADC, so it was never a test of this at all | static read of 15 files + HID/PAC/EM410x/Indala hardware rates | ⚠ CORRELATIONAL, n=2 failing protocols. The clean test is to move HID onto the shared path and re-measure the blue dual | ✓ the two families make a matched comparison the earlier one lacked | L60, L64, L69 |
| C48 | ⭐⭐⭐ **THE STRADDLE GATE CLOSES C39: on the front the decoder now produces 110 frames and 110 of them are correct.** Reject a frame when it is BOTH loud and ragged — `mean|integ| >= 2048` AND `min|integ| * 8 < mean|integ|`. Over 320 front captures it rejects **21 of 21** straddles and keeps 110 of 114 true frames (**40 of 40** at the phases `PHASE_ROTATION` actually uses), and over 320 back-side captures it changes **nothing** — 68 frames, 51 truth, exactly as before | 640 captures, both placements, shipping C decoder | ✓ the back-side set is the control: the gate must be inert there, and is | ✓ mirrored in compare.py; both decoders agree word-for-word on all 640 again | L61 |
| C49 | ⛔ **SHAPE ALONE IS NOT A GATE, AND THE OBVIOUS GATE IS WORSE THAN USELESS.** `min/mean` separates perfectly on the front (truth 0.36–0.62, straddles 0.001–0.092) and NOT AT ALL on the back, where true frames are also ragged (0.015–0.059): gating on shape alone keeps only 11 of 28 back-side reads at rotation phases. A 224-bit frame at RF/32 is 14336 samples, so the current shape (samples + scratch + two int32 accumulators) would need **168 KB**, and 112 KB even with int16 accumulators. Decoding in place with no stacking needs **28 KB**. ⇒ Indala224 is impossible at ANY stacking depth and comfortable without it. ⭐ And stacking buys nothing where it matters: 68.75% front with or without it, 32% vs 72% on the back — the placement the README tells users to avoid | link map + 320 committed captures | ✓ the no-stacking rate is the shipped decoder over single captures, re-run this session | ✓ the RAM figures come from the linker, not from the source | L90 |

A true alignment would be 128/128 and unique | 3 captures, 32 offsets, 128 rotations x 2 polarities | ✓ the lag sweep is its own control — 128 stands alone at 99.1% | ✓ the reference is the tag's memory dump and the Proxmark reads it correctly from the same placement | L100 |

| C113 | ⛔⛔ **THE TAG'S BLOCKS ARE A PERFECT PAC FRAME — so the air data is fine and MY DEMODULATION IS THE BUG.** Run `pac.c`'s own rules over `FF204990 6D8511C5 93155B56 D5B2649F` in software: the first 19 bits are **0x7F902, exactly `PAC_PREAMBLE`**; the twelve 10-bit UART frames starting at bit 8 all carry a valid start bit, odd parity and stop bit; they spell **STX '2' '0' C D 4 F 5 5 5 2** and the XOR of the eight card-ID bytes is **0x72**, which is exactly the twelfth byte. | C118 | ⭐⭐⭐ **THE LF PATH IS TRANSITION-SENSITIVE, WHICH IS WHY LEVEL DECODING CANNOT WORK FOR NRZ — and it explains every PAC error.** At the matched-filter alignment on a flat-on-pad capture, per-bit levels of KNOWN-1 bits depend on RUN LENGTH: isolated 1s read **10679**, runs of two **6791**, runs of three **5959**, runs of four or more **5422**. Known-0 bits sit at 2114 with a quarter of the spread. ⇒ The response is to CHANGES, not levels: an isolated bit is a sharp excursion while a long run decays toward the baseline, which is an AC-coupled front end doing what AC coupling does. ⭐ **Every error is in the long runs** — isolated 1s score 0 wrong out of 20 — and even a perfect threshold leaves 22 of 128 bits wrong, d-prime 2.31. ⚠ Harmless for PSK, which is constant-amplitude; fatal for NRZ. ⇒ `pac.c`'s edge-interval architecture is RIGHT and my level-threshold host decoder was the wrong model | 3 captures, 128 bits each | ✓ known-0 bits are the control and show none of the effect | ✓ the run-length grouping comes from the tag's memory, not from the capture | L106 |

The ASK family needed a new renderer, a drive sweep and a bit-rate parameter before its second protocol worked. ⚠ **Read only.** All three FSK writers are DEFERRED until the T5577 returns to the sandwich; the Proxmark can verify each then (`lf awid clone`, `lf paradox clone`, `lf pyramid clone`) | 4 + 2 + 4 + 3 device reads across 3 payloads and 1 null | ✓ the null is 0 of 2 after an explicit stop, and Pyramid's return leg carried a DIFFERENT payload the reader tracked | ✓ both frames match what the host decoder produced from independent captures, and the sources are the Flipper's own encoders | L162 |
| C199 | ⭐⭐ **FDX-A DECODES — the FSK family is complete on read, and its "failure" was my test payload, not the format.** FDX-A is the only protocol here with TWO encoding layers: FSK2a on the wire carrying MANCHESTER inside it, so bits 16..95 are 40 bit-pairs and its 80 carried bits hold 40 real ones. Its gate is the heaviest on the bench — a 16-bit preamble `0x551D`, the frame repeating at 96, **40 Manchester pair checks**, and odd parity on each of the five decoded bytes. ⛔ **The first attempt decoded perfectly and was rejected, correctly.** The Manchester layer was clean (0 illegal pairs of 40) and the bytes came back as exactly the emulated `01 02 03 04 05` — but two of them have EVEN parity, because **bit 7 of each FDX-A byte IS the odd-parity bit**, so an arbitrary 5-byte string is not a valid credential. Re-emulating `0102830485`, which satisfies the rule, decodes and passes: payload recovered exactly. ⭐ **The diagnosis came from printing the intermediate state, not from tuning**: the preamble was found at bits 37/133/229 with spacing exactly 96, which localised the rejection to `accept` before anything was changed. ⭐ **AND IT READS ON DEVICE: 4 of 4, null 0 of 2**, payload `0102830485` recovered exactly — so all four FSK protocols now read on hardware. ⚠ **26 cross-protocol nulls clean in both directions**, including the other three FSK protocols against FDX-A's capture and FDX-A against theirs | 2 payloads + 26 null captures + 320-capture regression | ✓ 26 nulls, 0 false positives, both directions | ✓ the invalid-parity payload is itself the null for the parity gate — it proves the check rejects a frame that is otherwise perfect | L163 |
| C200 | ⛔⛔ **A MISSING COMMAND ON `PATH` MASQUERADED AS A DEAD DEVICE FOR FOUR HOURS — the third instrument failure of this campaign and the most expensive.** Every check of the form `nrfutil device list | grep -c nordicDfu` returned 0, which I read as "no device entered DFU". `nrfutil` is not on this shell's `PATH` at all: the command failed and `grep -c` dutifully counted zero lines. With the real path — `.tools/bin/nrfutil`, which the flash script resolves explicitly and I did not — the same trigger shows **exactly one nordicDfu device**, and `nrfutil device program --traits nordicDfu` flashes the intended unit unambiguously. ⭐ **The targeted flash procedure works and is now proven**: trigger DFU on the chosen `/dev/cu.` port, confirm exactly ONE device in DFU (the other's normal port still present is the cross-check), then call nrfutil directly rather than the script, whose own the DFU trigger in `resource/tools/` picks whichever port the OS lists first. ⚠ **What WAS real: Chameleon #1 genuinely was wedged** — `hw mode` returned `CMD 1035 exec timeout`, which is an independent observation and needed the power cycle. The false part was the inference chain built on top of it. ⇒ **Three times now an instrument that could not express an outcome has reported its absence** — `flipper.py`'s one-word name matcher (C178), `framedrift.py`'s mismatched slicer (C180), and now a command that is not installed on the search path. ⭐ The common defence is the same each time: check the instrument against a KNOWN-GOOD case before trusting a negative result from it | 1 root cause, 4 hours lost, 1 successful targeted flash | ✓ the successful flash is the positive control — same trigger, same device, real path, one DFU device and a working result | ✓ `hw mode`'s timeout was independent of nrfutil and is what correctly identified the wedged device | L164 |
| C201 | ⭐⭐⭐ **THREE FSK READ ARMS UPGRADED FROM AN EMULATION TO A REAL TAG — 13 reads, every frame byte-identical to the Proxmark's own.** With the T5577 back in the sandwich, the Proxmark wrote each credential and our reader read the resulting tag: **AWID 5 of 5** (`011d81711dd1181111111111`, FC 12 / card 3456), **Paradox 4 of 4** (`0f55555695596a6a9999a59a`, FC 96 / card 40426), **Paradox and Pyramid each on a wiped tag**, **Pyramid 4 of 4** (`00010101010101010101016eb35e5da4`, FC 123 / card 11223). ⭐ **This is the evidence distinction the operator drew (C179), applied to the read side**: those three were previously verified only against a Flipper EMULATION, which is one decoder's rendering of the protocol; a T5577 written by the Proxmark is actual tag behaviour. Both agree exactly, which also retires any worry that the Flipper's emulation differed from a real tag for these formats. ⛔ **FDX-A CANNOT be upgraded: the Proxmark has no FDX-A clone command** — `lf fdx` is FDX-B, a different protocol — so like InstaFob it stays verified against a Flipper emulation alone, and the grid must not imply otherwise | 13 device reads across 3 protocols and 3 freshly written tags | ✓ each tag was written on a WIPED T5577 and confirmed by the Proxmark's own reader before ours was asked | ✓ the Proxmark is an independent writer AND reader; our frames match its raw output byte for byte in all three cases | L165 |
| C202 | ⭐⭐ **THE T5577 BLOCK FORM EQUALS THE AIR FRAME FOR ALL THREE FSK2a PROTOCOLS — asked per protocol, because the same question has the opposite answer for Keri.** Each of AWID, Paradox and Pyramid was cloned by a Proxmark and its blocks dumped: AWID `00107060 / 011D8171 1DD11811 11111111`, Paradox `00107060 / 0F555556 95596A6A 9999A59A`, Pyramid `00107080 / 00010101 01010101 0101016E B35E5DA4`. Every block form is **byte-identical** to the air frame our own decoder reads off that same tag, so the writer is a straight big-endian transcription. ⛔ **Keri's is not**: its block form is `(id << 3) | 7`, three bits out of phase with its air frame, and emitting the wrong one of the two put a stable, confidently-decoded, WRONG credential on the air 6 times out of 6 (C160). ⇒ One shared `fsk2a_t55xx_blocks()` serves all three, and a fourth FSK2a protocol does not get to use it until its own dump has been read. ⚠ The config words are the second measured constant: AWID and Paradox share `00107060` exactly, Pyramid differs only in the block-count field (4 blocks for its 128-bit frame) | 3 protocols, 1 clone-and-dump each | ⚠ **no null applies — this is a transcription identity, not a detection.** The CONTROL is Keri, the protocol where the same measurement comes back different, which is what makes the question non-vacuous | ✓ two independent sources per config word (`lf t55xx detect` on the real tag, and the Proxmark client's own header) and two per block form (the Proxmark's dump and our decoder's raw off the same tag) | L166 |
| C203 | ⭐⭐⭐ **THREE FSK WRITE ARMS VERIFIED THE STRONG WAY — we write the T5577, the PROXMARK reads it, 13 of 13, and the block dumps are identical to the Proxmark's own clone of the same credential.** Per protocol: the Proxmark cloned a reference credential and its blocks were dumped; the tag was **wiped** and the null confirmed; our writer wrote the frame from Chameleon #2; the Proxmark read it back. **AWID 5/5** (FC 34 / card 5678, `011db218271bd81111111111`), **Paradox 4/4** (FC 7 / card 12345, `0f55555556a5a555a965569a`), **Pyramid 4/4** (FC 45 / card 6789, `000101010101010101010164d0d51576`). ⭐ **Every credential is DIFFERENT from C201's**, so a stale tag cannot produce a pass — and the wipe means a silently-failed write reads as nothing rather than as the old value. ⭐ The dumps match the reference clone **block for block including block 0**, so this is identity with the reference, not merely a frame some reader happens to accept. ⭐ Bonus control: three FRESH credentials re-confirm C202's block-form-equals-air-frame result, taking it from n=3 to n=6. ⛔ **No FDX-A write arm, and that is a refusal rather than a deferral** — `lf fdx` is FDX-B, so nothing here can read an FDX-A tag we wrote and the arm would certify itself (C185) | 13 Proxmark reads of our writes, across 3 protocols and 3 wiped tags | ✓ each tag WIPED first and the protocol's own reader confirmed to find nothing before our write | ✓ the Proxmark is an independent reader AND the source of the reference blocks; our written tag is byte-identical to its own clone | L166 |
| C204 | ⭐⭐⭐ **GPROXII DECODES ON THE HOST, 96 of 96 BITS EXACT — and it needed a FOURTH decode path, because the level path this bench's whole ASK family runs on is physically unable to read RF/64.** ⛔ **Measured, not asserted: the LF front end is AC-coupled with a time constant of about 27 samples** — a step to 14112 decays to 4664 in 30 samples. At RF/32 a half-bit is 16 samples so the level survives to be sliced, which is why Gallagher, Securakey and Noralsy read. At RF/64 a half-bit is **32 samples, longer than the time constant**, so the level is gone before the half-bit ends — and sampling half-bit centres finds NO frame at any phase, low-pass width or inversion. ⭐ **The fix is to ask whether the envelope STEPPED, never what level it sits at.** Biphase puts a transition at every bit boundary by construction, so the boundaries are a self-calibrating amplitude reference and the only question per bit is whether the middle stepped too: `bit = 1` iff a mid-bit transition. One decision per bit, against a threshold this capture supplies itself. ⭐ **Graded by bit errors on a known credential, not pass/fail (M33)**: drive 7 **96/96**, drive 6 95/96, drive 4 84/96, drive 2 83/96 — which is C182's drive dependence appearing again, on a fourth protocol and a different decoder. ⚠ Drive 2 is the STOCK field and it clips: p5/p95 amplitude 4/16364 against drive 7's 2304/13536 | 4 captures, one credential, scored by bit error | ✓ the level path is the null and it is BLANK — no frame at any of 3 low-pass widths × 32 phases × 2 pairings × 2 inversions | ✓ the credential and its 96-bit frame come from the Proxmark's own `lf gproxii clone`; the frame period was independently measured by autocorrelation at **exactly 6144 samples** = 96 bits × RF/64 | L167 |
| C205 | ⭐⭐⭐ **THE CROSS-PROTOCOL NULL CAUGHT A READER THAT WOULD HAVE CLAIMED A SECURAKEY TAG WAS A GPROXII — STABLY, 3 CAPTURES OUT OF 3, SO TWO-AGREEING-STACKS WOULD HAVE CONFIRMED IT RATHER THAN REJECTED IT.** The firmware biphase decoder reads the real GProxII tag **4 of 4 exact** (`fac2a38c2b081af0210b12c2`). But gated only on what the Proxmark AND Momentum each check — a 6-bit preamble plus 18 always-zero spacers, 24 bits of 96 — it also produced frames on **Securakey 4/4, Noralsy 4/4, Gallagher 4/8, InstaFob 2/3, NexWatch 1/4**. ⭐ **The fix is the format's own length field**: strip the spacers, take the XOR key from the first byte, un-XOR the second, and its top six bits are the length — 26 on the bench tag, with key 141, which are exactly the `--fmt 26 --xor 141` we cloned, so the descramble is confirmed against values WE chose rather than by self-consistency. Both references compute this and both know only 26 and 36; neither REJECTS on it, because a single-protocol demod has nothing to be confused with and a multi-protocol reader does. ⭐ **With the length gate: Securakey 0/4, Noralsy 0/4, Gallagher 0/8, InstaFob 0/3, Keri 0/8, Indala224 0/4, IDTECK 0/16, AWID 0/6, PAC 0/4, empty 0/5, and 0 of 320 front captures.** ⚠ **NexWatch 1 of 4 SURVIVES and is reported rather than rounded away** — but it is sporadic where Securakey's was stable, which is the shape two-agreeing-stacks does catch. ⚠ The cost, said plainly: a genuine GProxII whose format length is neither 26 nor 36 would be rejected here | 386 captures across 11 protocols, before and after | ✓ the null IS the measurement here — 11 false positives before the length gate, 1 after | ✓ the true frame, its xor key and its format length all come from the Proxmark's own `lf gproxii clone` arguments | L168 |
| C206 | ⛔⛔ **THE SAME DECODER READS THE SAME TAG EXACTLY ON THE HOST AND WRONG BY ONE BIT ON THE DEVICE — open, and the arm is NOT graded.** `cdemod` decodes five `lf sniff --drive 7` captures taken FROM THIS DEVICE 5 of 5 byte-exact; the device's own reader returns `...12c6` where the tag holds `...12c2`, a persistent single-bit error at frame bit 93, on 7 reads of 8. ⚠ That bit falls outside format 26's facility and card fields, so the credential still prints correctly — which is why this is recorded as a FAILURE rather than a 7-of-8 success. Before the two fixes below, one read in 8 put the error INSIDE the card field and printed Card 1321 for a tag holding 1337. ⭐ **Two causes found and fixed, neither sufficient.** (1) `lf_ask_read`'s drive sweep starts at stock drive 4 and stops at the FIRST success — and drive 4 succeeds here, with errors, where the host measured drive 7 at 96/96 and drive 4 in the low 80s (C204); GProxII is now pinned to drive 7, not swept. (2) `raw_read_samples` returns SUCCESS on a capture that dropped ring samples — it checks only that the buffer filled, and a SPLICED buffer fills completely; the shared engine now discards those. ✅ Wrong-card reads went to 0 of 8; ⚠ that is n=8 against a ~12% rate and is NOT evidence the failure is gone. ⛔ **The bit-93 error survives both.** Ruled out by measurement, not argument: SAADC sample phase (8 values, all exact), settle time (6 values, all exact), sample width (the reader keeps the full 14 bits), and a stale host binary (rebuilt from scratch, still exact). ⇒ Something in the reader path still differs from the sniff path and it is not yet identified | 8 device reads + 19 host captures across 3 swept parameters | ✓ the host decode of captures from this same device IS the control, and it is exact | ✓ the tag's true frame is the Proxmark's own clone, `fac2a38c2b081af0210b12c2` | L169 |
| C207 | ⭐⭐ **THE GPROXII WRITE ARM IS VERIFIED WHILE ITS READ ARM IS NOT — our write, the PROXMARK's read, 4 of 4, block dump identical to the Proxmark's own clone.** Wrote `f84602a46119d4a114211046` (FC 45 / card 6789 / xor 200) from Chameleon #2 onto a WIPED tag whose null was confirmed; the Proxmark reads it back 4 of 4 and its blocks are `00150060 / F84602A4 6119D4A1 14211046`, byte-identical to what `lf gproxii clone` wrote for the same credential. ⭐ A different credential from the read tests, so a stale tag cannot pass. ⛔ **And our own reader reads that same tag 0 of 6** — the failure is now total where on the previous credential it was one bit, so it is credential-dependent rather than marginal. ✅ The host decodes `lf sniff` captures of this same tag 3 of 3 EXACT, taken by this same device minutes later: the decoder is right, the reader path is not (C206). ⭐ **Regression check on the spliced-capture discard: AWID still reads 5 of 5 exact**, so that change did not break the arms that were working | 4 Proxmark reads, 6 device reads, 3 host captures, 5 AWID regression reads | ✓ tag WIPED first and `lf gproxii reader` confirmed to find nothing before our write | ✓ the Proxmark is an independent reader AND the source of the reference blocks | L170 |
| C208 | ⭐⭐⭐ **C206'S MYSTERY IS THE PHASE ROTATION ORDER, NOT THE CAPTURE PATH — GProxII decodes on device at SAADC phase 112, which is SEVENTH of eight in the rotation, so the 3-second budget never reaches it.** With the budget raised to 8s and the device left in reader mode, it returns `f84602a46119d4a114211046` EXACTLY, at phase 112, after 4 to 8 tries. ⛔ The default read spends its whole budget in phases 20, 12, 28, 36, 44 and 16 — eight captures each — and either times out (0 of 6) or accepts a marginal decode from an early phase (the persistent bit-93 error). ⭐ **That explains every symptom in C206 at once**, including why the host was always exact: `lf sniff` captures at ONE phase and the host decoder has unlimited time, so it never had to reach the seventh entry. ⚠ **The rotation's own comment predicted this**: "THIS MAP IS ONE TAG, ONE UNIT, TWO COUPLING GEOMETRIES... it could move". It was measured on an Indala tag and 112 is in it only as SPREAD insurance, chosen for being far from the rest rather than for rank. ⚠ A second difference is measured and NOT explained: the reader path's edge energy is ~5050 where the same decoder on sniff captures reports 2300-2900, so the two paths really do see about twice the amplitude — it is not fatal, since the read succeeds, but it is not understood | 5 device reads at an 8s budget | ✓ two of the five still failed and reported their energy, which is what distinguished a bad capture from an unreached phase | ✓ the frame matches the Proxmark's own clone of the credential WE chose | L171 |
| C209 | ⛔⛔ **THE READER PATH MEASURES 1.8x THE SIGNAL THE SNIFF PATH DOES ON THE SAME TAG, SAME DEVICE, MINUTES APART — and that, not timing or phase order, is what stops the GProxII read.** Reader path: mean bit-boundary step **~5070 ADC counts on every failing read**, stable to a tenth of a percent. Sniff path, same tag, same minute: **2819**, and the host decodes it exactly. ⚠ A constant that large on every boundary is not tag envelope. ⛔ **Three configurations measured, best is 3 of 10**: phases {112,120,104,96,20,0} × 8 tries × 3s gave 3/10 exact and 0 wrong; {104,112,96,120,88} × 4 tries gave 0/12; {104,112,120,96,88,20} × 8 tries × **6s** gave 0/12. ⭐ **The 6s run is the one that matters: doubling the budget to reach five sample phases recovered nothing, which rules TIME out** — and C208's explanation with it. ✅ Ruled out by measurement, not argument: SAADC phase (16 values over 0..120, ALL decode on sniff), settle (6 values), gain (5 values), sample width, spliced captures, a stale host binary, and the bench itself (checked immediately after the 0-of-12: the Proxmark reads the tag and our own sniff decodes it). ✅ Both paths share `capture_begin`, `init_saadc_hw` and the same phase and gain setters, so the configuration is identical by inspection. ⇒ **Open. The read arm ships as research and is NOT graded; the write arm is unaffected and is verified (C207)** | 34 device reads across 3 configurations, 27 host captures across 3 parameter sweeps | ✓ the 6s arm IS the null for the timing hypothesis, and it came back blank | ✓ the Proxmark reads the tag throughout, and our own sniff capture decodes exactly on the host at every point in the sequence | L172 |
| C210 | ⭐⭐⭐ **THE READER'S OWN CAPTURES ARE FINE AT DRIVE 7 — SO THE READER IS NOT RUNNING AT DRIVE 7.** A new command runs the READER's capture path and hands the samples back undecoded, which is the one measurement `lf sniff` cannot make because sniff is the other path. Its captures, decoded on the host: **drive 7 phase 0 → exact, edge 2178; drive 7 phase 112 → exact, edge 2944; drive 4 phase 0 → NO FRAME, edge 7514.** ⭐ That kills the 1.8x mystery of C209 at a stroke: the reader path is not different from the sniff path at all, it is the same path at a different FIELD STRENGTH. An edge of 5000-7500 is the signature of drive 4, and the failing `lf gproxii read` reports ~5070. ⛔ **So `gproxii_read`'s `lf_125khz_radio_drive_set(7)` is not reaching most of its captures.** The probe sets the drive once and takes ONE capture and is clean; the real read sets it once and takes up to 40, each with its own `capture_begin`/`capture_end` cycle — and the two-agreeing-captures rule needs two good ones, which would explain a 3-in-10 success rate rather than none. ⚠ **Hypothesis, not yet confirmed**: the next test is setting the drive before EVERY capture rather than once per read | 3 probe captures at 2 drives and 2 phases | ✓ drive 4 IS the null and it reproduces the failure exactly, edge and all | ✓ the host decoder is the same binary that decodes sniff captures, and the frame matches the Proxmark's clone | L173 |
| C211 | ⭐⭐⭐ **THE SECOND BACK-TO-BACK CAPTURE IS DEGRADED, AND EVERY ONE AFTER IT — reproduced deterministically, and it is the whole of C206 through C210.** The reader-capture probe gained a `repeats` option: take N captures back to back, return the LAST. Same drive, same phase, same tag, one command: **repeats=1 → exact, edge 2266. repeats=2 → WRONG frame, edge 5033. repeats=4 and 8 → same wrong frame, edge ~5030. repeats=16 → badly wrong, edge 5027.** ⭐ 5030 is exactly the ~5047 every failing `lf gproxii read` reports, so the two are the same phenomenon. ⛔ **This retires the drive hypothesis (C210) and the phase-order one (C208) together**: the drive is re-asserted before every capture in this test and the phase is fixed at 112, and the degradation happens anyway. It is not the field strength and it is not which phase — it is BEING THE SECOND CAPTURE. ⚠ The two-agreeing-captures rule makes it fatal rather than merely lossy: capture 1 is clean and captures 2..N agree with each other on something WRONG, so the rule confirms the wrong answer or finds no pair at all. ⭐ It also explains why every other reader here is unaffected — they decode at RF/32 or on phase, where a degraded capture still slices; this decoder reads amplitude STEPS, which is what changes. ⇒ Next: whether a longer settle before each capture recovers it. `capture_begin` waits 2ms for the antenna AND the tag, and 2ms after a capture is not 2ms after a cold start | 5 probe runs at 1, 2, 4, 8 and 16 repeats | ✓ repeats=1 IS the control and it is exact every time it has been run — 14 of 14 across two sessions | ✓ the wrong frames still pass the 24-bit gate and the format-length check, which is why the device reported them confidently | L174 |
| C212 | ⭐⭐ **THE DEGRADED CAPTURE IS A CLIPPED ONE — and three fixes aimed at the wrong thing were tried and refuted.** Comparing the samples directly: a clean capture runs min 472 / max 14176, a capture taken second in a back-to-back run runs **min 0 / max 16380** — both rails, on a 14-bit ADC whose full scale is 16383. The percentiles are otherwise identical (p5 2332 vs 2328, median 3648 vs 3644, p95 13908 vs 13916), so it is not a level shift or a gain change: it is the PEAKS being cut off. ⭐ That is the mechanism, and it is specific to this decoder: it thresholds on step MAGNITUDE, which is exactly what clipping destroys — where the ASK family survives clipping because Manchester keeps the SIGN (C169, C171). ⛔ **Refuted, each by measurement:** (1) settle time — swept 0, 5, 10, 20, 50 and 100ms before every capture, all still degraded, so it is not the antenna or the tag settling; (2) a MEDIAN threshold instead of the mean — robust to clipped outliers in principle, and it broke the CLEAN captures too (2 of 4 bench captures wrong); (3) a LOCAL threshold from the two bracketing boundaries — 2 of 7 exact against the global mean's 7 of 7. ⚠ `capture_end` does call `stop_lf_125khz_radio()`, so the field IS dropped between captures; whatever stays charged, stays charged across that | 6 settle values, 2 threshold variants over 11 captures | ✓ the clean capture is the control in every comparison and it decodes exactly | ✓ the sample statistics come from the reader's OWN buffer, not a sniff, via the C210 probe | L175 |
| C213 | ⭐⭐⭐ **FIXED, AND THE GPROXII READ ARM IS VERIFIED: 12 of 12 EXACT, 0 WRONG, on a credential the Proxmark wrote and we chose.** The fix is a **field-off gap between captures**, and it is the one thing that worked after settle, threshold shape, drive, phase order, budget and try count had each been tried and refuted. Measured with the reader-capture probe, four captures back to back returning the last: **gap 0 and 5ms → wrong frame; 20 → wrong; 25, 30, 40 → exact but 35 came back one bit out; 50, 150, 250 → exact every time.** The threshold is around 25ms and soft near it, so the shipped value is **50ms, 2×**. ⭐ On device: `lf gproxii read` goes from **3 of 10** to **12 of 12 exact, 0 wrong**, at phase 112 in 2-3 tries. ⚠ **What is charging is NOT established** — `capture_end` already stops the field, so it is something holding charge across that: the tag's own storage, or the LF amplifier's AC coupling. What IS established is the symptom chain: too soon after another capture → CLIPS at both rails (C212) → step magnitudes saturate → this decoder, which thresholds on magnitude, fails. ⚠ **It costs latency and only this protocol pays it**: 114ms capture + 50ms gap means a 3s budget buys ~14 captures rather than 20. Every other reader passes 0 and is untouched | 12 device reads + 10 probe runs across 9 gap values | ✓ **cross-protocol nulls re-run on the shipping decoder**: Securakey 0/4, Noralsy 0/4, Gallagher 0/8, InstaFob 0/3, Keri 0/8, Indala224 0/4, IDTECK 0/16, AWID 0/6, PAC 0/4, empty 0/5, front 0/320 — and NexWatch 1/4, unchanged and still reported | ✓ the Proxmark wrote the credential and reads it back; our frame matches its raw byte for byte | L176 |
| C214 | ⭐⭐ **FDX-B DECODES ON THE HOST 4 of 4, AND ITS CROSS-PROTOCOL NULLS ARE COMPLETELY BLANK — 0 of 386, better than GProxII's.** The biphase decoder carried the second protocol as a format entry: 128 bits at RF/32, inverted, header `00000000001`, frame `00339a080402079f8040797788040201` (country 999 / national 1337). ⛔ **The 24-bit structural gate alone is WORTHLESS here and failed on the first capture tried**: a header of ten zeros and a one followed by 117 ONES satisfies every control bit trivially, and a drive-7 capture returned exactly that — `003fffffffffffffffffffffffffffff`. ⭐ **The real gate is a CRC-16 whose parameters were SOLVED against the bench frame, not copied**: the Proxmark reaches it through `crc16_fast(d, n, 0x0000, false, true)`, which reads as unreflected input and does NOT reproduce the tag's CRC; a search over poly / init / reflection / xor returns exactly one match, KERMIT (0x1021, init 0, reflected both ways), payload `39050000c0f90080` -> 0x1ED3, the value the tag carries. ⚠ **The CRC covers the first 8 bytes ONLY and the last 40 bits are unprotected** — already seen to matter: one capture in four returned `...88042a01` for a tag holding `...88040201`, gate satisfied. The Proxmark's own source remarks on it. ⚠ **Drive 4, not 7** — where GProxII needs 7 and decodes at nothing else; field strength is per protocol, a third time (C182, C204). ⛔ Capture length measured: 4608-8448 → 2 of 4, **8704-12288 → 4 of 4, and the FULL 14336 → 3 of 4** — the first constant here where longer is WORSE, so 10240 sits mid-plateau | 4 tag captures, 4 drives, 13 truncation lengths, 386 null captures | ✓ **nulls blank across 12 protocol sets, 5 empty and all 320 front captures** — no exceptions, unlike GProxII's NexWatch 1/4 | ✓ the frame, its payload and its CRC all come from the Proxmark's own clone of a credential we chose | L177 |
| C215 | ⭐⭐⭐ **FDX-B COMPLETE ON HARDWARE, BOTH DIRECTIONS — read 6 of 6 on device, write 4 of 4 by the Proxmark — and it needed NONE of GProxII's special handling.** Read: `lf fdxb read` returns `00339a080402079f8040797788040201`, country 999 / national 1337 / animal, 6 times of 6. Write: our writer put `0031bd39740201f8804039b518040201` (country 124 / national 7654321) on a WIPED tag whose null was confirmed, and the Proxmark reads it back 4 of 4 with **CRC-16 ok** and block 0 `00098080`, identical to its own clone. A different credential from the read tests, so a stale tag cannot pass. ⭐⭐ **The second protocol of a family is where you find out whether it IS a family, and this one is — but not by inheritance.** Measured rather than assumed: FDX-B reads at the STOCK drive where GProxII needs 7 and decodes at nothing else, and four back-to-back captures with NO gap decode exactly where GProxII's second capture is already wrong (C211). ⇒ it uses the plain shared reader and GProxII's overrides stay GProxII's. ⚠ Its config is DIPHASE where GProxII is BIPHASE — one bit apart, and the wrong one writes a tag nothing here could read | 6 device reads, 4 Proxmark reads, 2 credentials | ✓ tag WIPED and `lf fdxb reader` confirmed blank before our write | ✓ the Proxmark is an independent reader AND the source of the reference blocks; the host field decoder returns the country, national and animal values WE chose | L178 |
| C216 | ⭐⭐ **THE FIRST FSK2a EMITTER ROUND-TRIPS EXACTLY ON THE HOST — emitter to air to decoder, both halves the shipping firmware.** AWID's 96-bit frame `011db218271bd81111111111` emits as 543 PWM entries and our own decoder reads it back byte-exact. ⭐ **FSK2a emulation is one PWM entry per TONE PERIOD, not per bit**, which is the whole difference from the ASK emitters: a 0 is six entries of `counter_top` 8 (RF/8) and a 1 is five of 10 (RF/10). ⚠ **Those are 48 and 50 carrier cycles — NOT the same length** — so anything on this family that assumes a constant bit period is wrong by up to 4%. ⚠ It is also the first emitter here whose ENTRY COUNT depends on the data (543 for this frame, 480-576 possible), so the sequence length is computed per call where every ASK emitter's is fixed at compile time. ⭐ The tone-to-bit mapping came from the shipping DECODER's own `pulses_short`/`pulses_long`, not a datasheet — emitting what our reader counts is exactly what the round trip tests. ✅ `render_ask()` renders it unchanged, and that is not a shortcut: it emits `counter_top` samples per entry, first half high, which is one Manchester bit for an ASK entry and one tone cycle for an FSK one. The peripheral really does treat them the same | 1 emitter, 1 frame, host only — NOT yet on hardware | ✓ **sensitivity proven by a deliberate break**: swapping the two pulse counts gives 513 entries and NO DECODE | ✓ the decoder half is the same binary that reads real AWID tags 5 of 5 (C201) | L179 |
| C217 | ⛔⛔ **THE AWID EMULATION IS SILENT TO THE FLIPPER — 0 of 6 — AND THE POSITIVE CONTROL PASSES 6 of 6 MINUTES LATER ON THE SAME RIG.** Chameleon #1 emulating AWID `011db218271bd81111111111`: the Flipper reads nothing, on both its ASK and PSK arms. Switch the SAME slot to Gallagher `7feaa31e76d86c6d868cc249` and the Flipper reads it **6 of 6** with the right credential. ⇒ the rig, the coupling and the emulation path are all working; the FSK emission specifically is not being read. ⭐ **This is why F05's no-positive-control rule exists**: the first result on its own was a null that could equally have meant a dead rig, and `flipper.py` said so in as many words before the control was run. ⚠ **And it does NOT refute C216**: the emitter round-trips through our own shipping decoder exactly, so what the PWM is asked to emit decodes. What is not established is whether the PERIPHERAL emits it — this is the first emitter here to use `counter_top` 8 and 10 where every other uses 32, 40 or 64, and the first whose sequence length is computed at runtime. ⛔ **Nothing on this bench can capture rig A's emission**: both Chameleons are on separate rigs and the Flipper has no raw-capture path here, so the next step needs either the two Chameleons facing each other or a scope | 6 Flipper reads + 6 control reads, same rig, same session | ✓ **the control IS the null's null** — Gallagher 6/6 on the same slot minutes later | ✓ the emitter's own round trip against the shipping decoder is exact, and a deliberate break fails it (C216) | L180 |
| C218 | ⚠⚠ **THE AWID EMITTER DOES PRODUCE BOTH TONES — which CORRECTS C217's lead — but the long one is not clean, and this measurement has NO CONTROL so it is reported as a lead, not a finding.** 10,784 pulse periods from the Flipper's raw capture of our emission: **~62-65 carries 7,716 of them (mode 63) and ~73-79 carries 1,678 (spread, centred ~76)**. The ratio 76/63 = 1.21 against the 10/8 = 1.25 the protocol wants, so both tones ARE there and roughly where they belong. ⛔ **C217's single-tone reading is retracted**: it was 24 pairs eyeballed out of 72,518 and the second tone simply was not in them. ⭐ What the histogram DOES show is an asymmetry: the short tone is TIGHT (63 ± 1, 74% of its mass on one value) and the long one is BROAD (73-79 with no dominant value where 79-80 is expected). A tone that measures 2-8% short and scattered is a plausible reason for a decoder to reject it. ⛔⛔ **AND THE CONTROL COULD NOT BE TAKEN**: the Gallagher run that should have calibrated the instrument returned ZERO periods twice, on a protocol the same rig reads 6 of 6. So the instrument is uncalibrated and these numbers cannot be promoted past a lead. ⚠ **`rfid raw_analyze` over the CLI is NOT a usable instrument here and should not be tried again** — it has now left the Flipper's CLI unresponsive twice, once badly enough to drop the port | 10,784 periods, 1 protocol, NO control | ⛔ **the control is BLANK and that is the finding's own limit** — Gallagher 0 periods twice where it reads 6/6 | ⚠ none available: the Flipper is the only reader that can see rig A's emission | L182 |
| C219 | ⭐⭐⭐ **THE FLIPPER'S OWN THRESHOLD SAYS WHY IT REJECTS OUR AWID: 909 ANOMALOUS PERIODS LAND JUST ABOVE ITS SHORT/LONG BOUNDARY, AND THEY OUTNUMBER THE GENUINE LONG TONES 909 TO 613.** Read out of Momentum's source rather than guessed: `protocol_awid.c` builds `fsk_demod_alloc(64-20, 6, 80+20, 5)`, so a period counts only inside **[44, 100)** and is called LONG at or above **mid = 72**. ⭐ Calibrating the Flipper's time unit from our own emission — 8 carrier cycles (64us) measures 63 units — puts its true long tone at **~78.7 units**, and the histogram's **77-79 cluster (613 periods)** is exactly that. ⛔ **But 73-75 carries 909 periods and nothing should be there.** They sit above mid=72, so every one is counted as a LONG pulse; with `hi_pulses = 5` a handful of spurious longs is enough to corrupt the bit the group decodes to. ⚠ **Mechanism is a hypothesis and is labelled one**: a hybrid period at each tone-group boundary, as would happen if the PWM applies a new `counter_top` one period late. The count fits — ~96 group boundaries per frame over ~17 captured frames is the right order — but nothing here has confirmed the peripheral behaves that way. ✅ What IS established: AWID is registered in Momentum with `LFRFIDFeatureASK`, so `rfid read ask` does try it, and the protocol's own acceptance window is what our emission falls foul of | 10,784 periods, 1 protocol, no control (C218) | ⚠ inherits C218's missing control — the WINDOW arithmetic is from source and is solid, the period counts are not calibrated | ✓ the thresholds are Momentum's own constants, not our reading of the waveform | L183 |
| C220 | ⭐⭐⭐ **MOMENTUM'S OWN DEMODULATOR DECODES OUR EMITTER'S IDEAL OUTPUT EXACTLY — so what the emitter ASKS FOR is not the problem, and the fault is what the peripheral EMITS.** Two explanations survived C217 and no instrument on this bench separated them: (a) the PWM does not emit what the emitter asks for, or (b) what it asks for is not what the Flipper accepts. ⭐ **(b) is decidable without hardware**: render the emitter's PWM entries as the level/duration pairs they describe, with NO hardware artefacts, and run Momentum's `fsk_demod_feed` over them. `momdemod.py` transcribes that function and AWID's own `fsk_demod_alloc(64-20, 6, 80+20, 5)`, and our ideal emission yields **383 bits containing `011db218271bd81111111111` exactly**. ⇒ **(b) is dead by elimination and (a) stands.** ⭐ It also corroborates C219 from the other side: if the design is right and the emission is rejected, the 909 anomalous periods at 73-75 are hardware artefacts rather than a design error — which is what C219's one-period-lag hypothesis predicted. ⚠ **A transcription, not a resemblance**, and deliberately: a demodulator that merely resembled the reference would prove nothing about it, which is what `framedrift.py` cost a day to learn (C180) | 4 frames rendered ideally, 383 bits | ✓ the demodulator is Momentum's algorithm and its constants, transcribed from `fsk_demod.c` and `protocol_awid.c` | ✓ the emitter half is the SHIPPING firmware's own PWM entries, the same ones ctest/roundtrip.c renders | L184 |
| C221 | ⭐⭐ **THE GPROXII BIPHASE EMITTER ROUND-TRIPS EXACTLY — and TWO deliberate breaks FAILED TO FAIL IT, each for a reason worth having.** 192 PWM entries, two per bit, decoded byte-exact by the shipping biphase decoder. ⛔ **Break 1, inverting which symbol carries the mid-bit transition: still exact.** That is not a broken test — the decoder sweeps inversion by design, because real biphase tags differ in polarity and `lf t55xx detect` reports it per tag. A global convention flip is genuinely indistinguishable. ⛔ **Break 2, removing the bit-boundary transition ENTIRELY: still exact.** This one is a statement about OUR DECODER and it is worth recording: `lf_ask_biphase.c` asks one question per bit — did the middle step — and uses the boundaries only as a threshold reference. Strip them and the mid-bit steps still fall exactly on the 1-bits, so the answer is unchanged. ⚠ **⇒ a signal that is not biphase at all decodes identically**, and what stops that mattering is the format gate (preamble + spacers + format length, C205), not the decoder. ⭐ The emitter uses `counter_top` 32 — the same magnitude the working ASK emitters use, against AWID's 8 and 10. If it reads on the Flipper where AWID does not, that localises the AWID defect to small counter_tops; if it does not read, the fault is broader than FSK (C217, C220) | 1 emitter, 1 frame, 2 breaks, host only | ⚠ **the sensitivity control is WEAKER than AWID's and that is said rather than glossed** — AWID's arm fails on a pulse-count swap, this one survived both breaks tried | ✓ the decoder half reads real GProxII tags 12 of 12 on device (C213) | L185 |
| C222 | ⛔⛔ **THE COUNTER_TOP HYPOTHESIS IS REFUTED: GProxII uses 32, the same as the working Gallagher, and the Flipper reads it 0 of 6 too — with the control at 6 of 6.** The experiment C221 was built for, run on the same slot in one session: **Gallagher 6/6, AWID 0/6, GProxII 0/6.** ⇒ whatever silences our two new emitters, it is not the magnitude of `counter_top`. ⭐ **One structural difference survives: every emitter that WORKS stores exactly ONE PWM entry per bit, and both that fail store several.** em410x, Gallagher, Securakey, Noralsy, Viking, Jablotron and PAC are all one-entry-per-bit at the 125kHz clock; AWID stores five or six and GProxII stores two. ⚠ That is a correlation across seven working and two failing protocols, not a mechanism — and the obvious candidates do NOT fit: GProxII's entry RATE is identical to Gallagher's (32 ticks each), and sequence LENGTH is not it either, since Indala224 plays 448 entries and reads 11 of 11 (C152). ⚠ A second difference applies to GProxII alone: its entries hold a LEVEL (duty 0 or duty == counter_top) where every working emitter emits a 50% square. ⛔ **Two failures, two possible causes, and this bench cannot see the emission** — the instrument that would separate them is still the one in Needs hands | 18 Flipper reads across 3 protocols, one session, one slot | ✓ **the control is the point of the experiment and it passed twice** — Gallagher 6/6 before and after | ✓ both emitters round-trip exactly through our own decoders, and Momentum's own demodulator accepts AWID's ideal output (C220) | L186 |
| C223 | ⛔⛔ **ENTRIES-PER-BIT IS REFUTED TOO — GProxII rebuilt as ONE entry per bit is still 0 of 6, control 6 of 6 — and ONE structural difference now survives: every working emitter emits a 50% SQUARE IN EVERY ENTRY.** The two-way test C222 set up, run: the emitter was re-expressed as one PWM entry per bit (96 instead of 192) with nothing else changed, and it reads exactly the same nothing. ⭐ **The table after four measurements:** Gallagher 1 entry/bit, top 32, every entry a square → **6/6**; AWID 5-6 entries/bit, top 8 and 10, squares → 0/6; GProxII v1 2 entries/bit, top 32, HELD levels → 0/6; GProxII v2 1 entry/bit, top 64, half squares and half HELD → 0/6. ⇒ entries-per-bit dead, counter_top magnitude dead (em410x uses 64 and works). ⭐ **What is left for GProxII is the held level** — duty 0 or duty == counter_top, no switching inside the entry at all — and it has a mechanism: the emulation modulates by switching a load, and the reader front end this branch MEASURED as AC-coupled with a ~27-sample time constant (C204) cannot see a level that never changes. ⛔ **AWID stays unexplained**: every one of its entries IS a 50% square, at periods of 64 and 80us, comfortably inside that passband. ⇒ two failures, and now only one of them has a candidate cause | 24 Flipper reads across 4 emitter builds, 3 protocols | ✓ **the control passed on every one of the four runs** — Gallagher 6/6 each time, same slot, same session | ✓ every emitter round-trips exactly through our own decoders, and Momentum's own demodulator accepts AWID's ideal output (C220) | L187 |
| C224 | ⛔ **AC COUPLING IS RULED OUT FOR AWID — its emission still decodes through Momentum's own demodulator at every high-pass time constant tried, down to 20 samples.** The mechanism proposed for GProxII's silence (C223) was that a held level cannot modulate a load-switching emulator seen through an AC-coupled front end. AWID holds nothing — all its entries are 50% squares — so the same mechanism should spare it, and the sweep confirms that: rendered to samples, high-passed at tau of 2000, 1000, 500, 200, 100, 50 and 20 samples, then re-thresholded to edges, **the frame is still found at every one**. ⚠ **AND THE MODEL CANNOT TEST THE GPROXII HALF OF THE CLAIM, which is said here rather than quietly enjoyed**: the high-pass decays toward zero and the edge detector thresholds AT zero, so a held level decays asymptotically and never crosses — it survives as a clean long run at any tau. A real comparator has hysteresis and a baseline and a decayed level DOES vanish into it. ⇒ the model says something about emissions made of squares and NOTHING about emissions containing held levels, and the GProxII row it prints is not evidence | 7 time constants × 3 emitters, compute only | ✓ tau=0 is the control row and it decodes, matching C220 | ⚠ the time constant is NOT measured for the Flipper's front end — C204's ~27 samples was the Chameleon's receiver, a different circuit — so this is a sensitivity analysis and its useful output is that AWID survives the whole range | L188 |
| C225 | ⛔ **THE PROXMARK CANNOT STAND IN FOR THE FLIPPER WHILE THE TAG IS IN THE SANDWICH — with Chameleon #2 emulating, it reads NEITHER the emulation NOR the tag.** An attempt to get a second, independent reader onto our emissions without hands: flash #2, emulate **Gallagher** (the protocol the Flipper reads 6/6), and ask the Proxmark. **0 of 4.** And `lf fdxb reader` in the same state also returns nothing, on a tag it reads perfectly the moment #2 goes back to reader mode. ⇒ the two sources modulate the same field and take each other out, exactly as the bench notes predicted when the lift was first requested (C179). ⭐ **Recorded so the next session does not spend a flash cycle rediscovering it**: with the T5577 in place, rig B has no emulation-verification path at all, in either direction. ⚠ It also rules out the boring explanations for the silent emitters on the way: the emulated frame reads BACK correctly through `econfig` for AWID as well as Gallagher, so the buffer plumbing, the tag-type registration and `tag_emulation_load_by_buffer` are all working — the data reaches the emulator | 4 Proxmark reads of the emulation + 2 of the tag, before and after | ✓ **the after-reading IS the control**: the tag reads 124-000007654321 the moment #2 stops emulating, so nothing was broken | ✓ Gallagher was chosen deliberately as the arm the Flipper DOES read, so a null here is about the geometry and not the emitter | L189 |
| C226 | ⭐⭐ **CAPTURED A REAL, WORKING FSK2a EMISSION ON THIS BENCH — the Flipper emulating AWID, sniffed by Chameleon #1 and decoded byte-exact by our own reader — and it does NOT use a 50% duty.** The instrument was there all along and I had written the rig off: rig A runs BOTH ways, so with the Flipper as the SOURCE, Chameleon #1's `lf sniff` is a reference-grade capture of a working emission. ⭐ **Its structure, 1,668 periods: the HIGH run is 4 samples on 1,207 of them regardless of tone, and only the LOW run varies — 4 for the RF/8 tone and 6 for RF/10.** Periods come out 8 and 10 as expected, but the mark is FIXED and the gap carries the data. Our emitter used 5 high / 5 low for the long tone, which sums to the same period and which our own decoder reads perfectly, because it only ever looks at the sum. ⛔ **Matching the measured duty did NOT fix it: still 0 of 6, control 4 of 4.** So duty is not the cause either — the fourth hypothesis to fall. ⭐ The change is KEPT regardless: a real tag shorts its coil for a fixed time, 50% duty leaves the field loaded 25% longer than any real AWID tag at the long tone, and guessing differently from the only working emission on this bench needs a reason we do not have. ✅ **The capture is committed** under `caps/awid-flipper-emit/` as the reference any future emitter work compares against | 1,668 periods from one reference emission; 6 Flipper reads of ours, control 4/4 | ✓ our decoder reads the reference emission byte-exact, so the capture is a valid recording of a working AWID | ✓ the control ran after the test on the same slot | L190 |
| C227 | ⛔⛔ **RETRACTED BY C228 — THE INPUT WAS WRONG.** ~~**THE BIPHASE PATH FAILS IN BOTH DIRECTIONS BETWEEN THESE TWO DEVICES — our decoder reads real GProxII T5577 tags 12 of 12 and reads the FLIPPER's GProxII emulation 0 of 4.** Captured with the Flipper as source and Chameleon #1 sniffing, the same rig and method that produced a byte-exact AWID reference minutes earlier (C226). Four drives tried, 2 / 4 / 6 / 7: nothing at any of them. ⭐ **That reframes the problem, and the table is the finding:** ASK/Manchester works BOTH ways (Gallagher: our emitter read by the Flipper 6/6, real tags read by us); FSK2a works ONE way (the Flipper's AWID emission decodes byte-exact for us, ours is silent to it); **biphase works NEITHER way** even though both ends read real T5577 tags of that protocol. ⚠ So this is not simply "our emitter is wrong" — whatever differs, it differs symmetrically, and a real tag satisfies both devices where neither device satisfies the other. ✅ The emission IS present and structured: sliced as levels it runs 29-35 samples (half-bit 32) with longer runs at 66 and 96, so the Flipper is emulating and the capture is real. ⚠ Runs of 96 are THREE half-bits, which biphase forbids — but that is measured through a level slicer, and C204 established that levels decay at this bit rate, so it is not evidence about the emission | 4 captures across 4 drives, 1 protocol | ✓ **the AWID capture from the identical setup decodes byte-exact**, so the source, the rig and the capture path are all sound | ✓ our decoder reads real GProxII tags 12 of 12 with 0 wrong (C213) | L191~~ ⇒ see C228 | L191, retracted in L192 |
| C228 | ⛔⛔ **C227 IS RETRACTED AND THE ERROR WAS MINE: I FED THE FLIPPER THE WRONG BYTES.** Momentum's GProxII ENCODER reads `protocol->data` — the full 96-bit frame, preamble and spacers included — where its DECODER writes `protocol->decoded_data`, the 90 bits after the preamble. I passed the decoded form, so the Flipper faithfully emitted a frame with no valid preamble, and our reader found nothing because there was nothing to find. ✅ **Given the frame it actually wants, our decoder reads the Flipper's GProxII emulation 3 of 4 EXACT** (`f84602a46119d4a114211046`), captured through the reader path at drive 7. ⭐ **The corrected table is cleaner and points one way:** ASK both directions; FSK2a Flipper→us ✓, us→Flipper ✗; biphase Flipper→us ✓ (3/4), us→Flipper ✗. ⇒ **every failure is in the SAME direction — our emission into the Flipper — and every Flipper emission decodes for us.** That is a sharper statement than C227's symmetry and it puts the fault on our emitter side, where four hypotheses have already been eliminated. ⚠ **How this slipped through**: the decoded and encoded forms are both 12 bytes, so nothing rejected the input and the null looked like a finding. A length check would not have caught it; only reading the encoder would have, which is what should have happened first | 4 reader-path captures, 1 protocol | ✓ the wrong-input capture is the null and it contains no preamble at ANY phase, threshold or inversion — 60 hits on a real tag, 0 on that one | ✓ the frame matches what the Flipper was told to emulate | L192 |
| C229 | ⭐⭐⭐ **BOTH SILENT EMITTERS ARE NOW SHOWN CORRECT BY REFERENCE TO MOMENTUM'S OWN CODE — not by assertion — so the fault is the peripheral's realisation and this bench cannot observe it.** For AWID that was C220: Momentum's own `fsk_demod_feed`, transcribed, decodes our emitter's ideal output exactly. For GProxII it is the encoder itself: `protocol_gproxii_encoder_yield` emits **two SHORT pulses of 32 for a 1 and one LONG pulse of 64 for a 0**, toggling the level on every yield — which is precisely the waveform both our versions describe. v1 held a level across each half-bit (32+32 for a 1, one 64 run for a 0); v2 expresses the same thing as one entry with duty 32 or a held level. **Structurally identical to the reference, in both versions.** ⭐ **So the emitter question is closed as far as source can close it**: design verified against the reference implementation for one protocol by its DECODER and for the other by its ENCODER, and both are silent on the air. ⛔ Five hypotheses are now dead — counter_top magnitude, entries per bit, AC coupling, duty shape, and the design itself. ⚠ What remains is unobservable here: the only reader that can hear rig A's emission is the Flipper, and it is the thing under test. ⇒ **the tag lift is no longer a nice-to-have, it is the only way this advances** | source reading + every measurement from C216 onward | ✓ the Flipper's own emissions of BOTH protocols decode for us — AWID byte-exact, GProxII 3 of 4 — so the capture path and our decoders are not in question | ✓ Gallagher, the control, reads 6/6 in the same direction our two fail | L193 |
| C230 | ✅ **THE BIPHASE FAMILY'S NULL IS NOW CLEAN IN BOTH DIRECTIONS — the half I had not run comes back completely blank.** Rule 11 asks for both, and until now only one had been done: the GProxII and FDX-B decoders against everyone else's captures (C205, C214). This is the converse — **eleven other decoders against the biphase captures**: Keri, NexWatch, Gallagher, Securakey, Noralsy, InstaFob, AWID, Paradox, Pyramid, FDX-A and Indala224, plus plain Indala and IDTECK. **Not one produces a frame on either the GProxII or the FDX-B tag captures.** ⭐ Worth having rather than assumed: the ASK/Manchester decoders in particular slice the same amplitude these tags modulate, and Securakey's entire gate is 19 preamble bits with no payload check (C175) — it is exactly the decoder most likely to claim a frame it should not | 11 decoders × 12 captures | ✓ the null IS the measurement here and it is blank with no exceptions | ✓ every one of those decoders reads its own protocol on device, so none of them is blank because it is broken | L194 |
| C231 | ⭐⭐ **EIGHT T5577 WRITERS NOW HAVE HOST COVERAGE, UP FROM THREE — AND KERI'S ROTATION IS PINNED.** C202 flagged that six writers had no host test at all; five of those had their reference block dumps already written down in the tree and unused, so the arms cost typing rather than bench time. Added: **Keri, Gallagher, NexWatch, GProxII and FDX-B**, each against the blocks a real Proxmark clone was recorded leaving on a tag. ⭐ **Keri is the one that matters.** Its block form is `(id << 3) | 7` — three bits out of phase with the air frame it is handed — and sending the frame view where the block form belongs gave a stable, confident, WRONG credential 6 times out of 6 (C160), with nothing on the host able to see it. Now there is: removing the rotation makes the arm print `00000000 80003039` against the wanted `00000004 000181CF` and fail. ✅ **Sensitivity proven by that deliberate break**, not assumed from a green tick — which matters after the GProxII emitter arm survived two breaks (C221). ⚠ Securakey and Noralsy still have none: their reference dumps were never recorded, and inventing expected blocks from the writer's own code would test nothing | 8 writer arms, 1 deliberate break | ✓ the break IS the control and it fails with a printed diff | ✓ every expected block set comes from a Proxmark clone recorded in the tree, not from our own writer | L195 |
| C232 | ✅ **EVERY T5577 WRITER IN THE TREE NOW HAS HOST COVERAGE — ten arms, and the last two needed a measurement rather than a search.** C231 had to leave Securakey and Noralsy out because nobody had ever written their block forms down, and inventing the expected blocks from our own writer's code would have tested nothing. Two Proxmark clones later: **Securakey `000C8060 / 7FCB4000 01ADEA53 44300000`** and **Noralsy `00088068 / BB0214FF 01100022 33070000`**, both now recorded in `t55xx.h` beside the config words that previously cited a dump nobody could see. ⭐ **Both are straight transcriptions — block form equals air frame — which is what makes the arms cheap and is exactly the thing that had to be checked rather than assumed**, because Keri's is not and that cost a confident wrong credential 6 of 6 (C160). ✅ Ten writers, ten arms: AWID, Paradox, Pyramid, Keri, Gallagher, NexWatch, GProxII, FDX-B, Securakey, Noralsy | 2 clone-and-dump measurements, 10 arms | ✓ the Keri arm's deliberate break (C231) is the sensitivity control for the whole family of arms | ✓ every expected block set comes from a Proxmark clone, never from our own writer | L196 |
| C233 | ⭐⭐ **THE THREE ASK WRITE ARMS ARE UPGRADED TO THE EVIDENCE STANDARD THE NEWER ONES SET — wiped tag, confirmed null, 4 of 4 each.** Gallagher, Securakey and Noralsy were graded "3/3 via Proxmark" months of work ago, before the tag was routinely WIPED first and before the credential was routinely CHANGED — the discipline C203 introduced for the FSK writers and C207/C215 for the biphase ones. Re-run to that bar: **Gallagher 4/4** (region 2 / facility 1234 / card 5678 / issue 3, a different credential from the old region 1 / 4321 / 6789 / 2), **Noralsy 4/4** (card 445566 / year 2015, changed from 112233 / 2000), **Securakey 4/4** (`7FCB400001ADEA5344300000`). Each on a tag wiped immediately before, with the protocol's own Proxmark reader confirmed to find NOTHING first. ⚠ **Securakey's credential is NOT changed and cannot be**: `lf securakey clone` takes only a raw frame and nothing here encodes one, so its evidence rests on the wipe alone. Said rather than glossed — the wipe does the work a changed credential would, but it is one leg instead of two | 12 Proxmark reads across 3 protocols, 3 wipes | ✓ **the wipe-and-null is the control for each**, run immediately before every write | ✓ the Proxmark is an independent reader and the source of every reference credential | L197 |
| C234 | ⭐⭐⭐ **KERI'S ROTATION IS NOW VERIFIED END TO END ON HARDWARE, AND NEXWATCH WITH IT — every write arm on this branch is at the same evidence standard.** Keri matters most: its block form is `(id << 3) | 7` and getting it wrong gave a stable, confident, WRONG credential 6 times out of 6 (C160). Our writer, given internal id **8000D431** (card 54321, changed from the old 12345), on a tag wiped immediately before with `lf keri reader` confirmed blank, produces **`603E1040 / 00000004 0006A18F` — byte-identical to the Proxmark's own clone of the same credential**, and the Proxmark reads it back **4 of 4**. ⭐ The rotation is therefore pinned twice over: on the host by `ctest` with a deliberate break (C231), and on hardware against a reference clone here. NexWatch likewise: card **87654321** (changed from 12345678), blocks `00081060 / 56000000 0012776A 2F1AF000` identical to its clone, 4 of 4. ✅ **All ten write arms now carry: wiped tag, confirmed null, independent reader, and — for nine of ten — a credential different from the previous test.** Securakey is the one exception and only because nothing here can encode a second frame for it (C233) | 8 Proxmark reads across 2 protocols, 2 wipes | ✓ the wipe-and-null ran immediately before each write | ✓ the block dumps are compared against the Proxmark's own clone of the SAME credential, not against our writer's own output | L198 |
| C235 | ⭐⭐⭐ **THE LAST FIVE READ ARMS UPGRADED FROM AN EMULATION TO A REAL TAG — 25 reads, every frame byte-identical to the Proxmark's own raw, and four of the five on a credential I chose.** C179 is the operator's distinction and C201 applied it to the three FSK arms; these five were still graded against a Flipper EMULATION, which is one decoder's rendering of a protocol rather than tag behaviour. Re-run against T5577s the Proxmark wrote: **NexWatch 5/5** (card 87654321), **Keri 5/5** (internal id 80007A69, card 31337), **Gallagher 5/5** (region 3 / facility 999 / card 4242 / issue 1), **Noralsy 5/5** (card 778899 / year 2022), **Securakey 5/5** (`7fcb400001adea5344300000`). ⭐ **Every credential is new to this bench**, so none of these is a reader returning a value it has seen before — except Securakey, where nothing here can encode a second frame and which is flagged for that in the grid. ✅ **With C201 this completes the upgrade: every read arm on the branch that CAN be tested against a real tag now has been.** The two that cannot — FDX-A and InstaFob — stay emulation-verified and the grid says so | 25 device reads across 5 protocols, 5 freshly written tags | ✓ each tag was written and confirmed by the Proxmark's own reader before ours was asked | ✓ the Proxmark is an independent writer AND reader; our frames match its raw byte for byte in all five cases | L199 |
