The corpus had not followed. ✅ Added `caps/fsk-realtag/`: three captures each of **AWID** (FC 77 / card 16180, `011d84747e8d811111111111`), **Paradox** (FC 21 / card 31415, `0f5555556666a999a6a9aa6a`) and **Pyramid** (FC 88 / card 27182, `00010101010101010101016b8651babc`), all taken through the READER path with `rdrcap.py` and all decoding byte-identical to the Proxmark's own raw. ⭐ **Every credential is new to this bench**, so the corpus is not three more recordings of a frame the decoders have already memorised. ⛔ **FDX-A gets none and cannot**: the Proxmark has no FDX-A clone, so its only possible capture is of an emulation — the same limit that keeps its write arm unshipped (C185) | 9 captures, 3 protocols, 3 freshly written tags | ✓ **cross-protocol nulls over the new corpus are blank** — GProxII, FDX-B, Gallagher, Securakey, Noralsy, Keri and NexWatch all find nothing in any of the 9 | ✓ each tag was written and confirmed by the Proxmark's own reader first | L200 |
| C237 | ⭐⭐ **THE MEASURED DUTY IS NOW PINNED, AND THE BREAK PROVES THE ROUND TRIP COULD NEVER HAVE CAUGHT IT.** C226 measured a real AWID emission and found the mark FIXED at 4 samples with only the gap varying; our emitter was emitting 5 and 5 for the long tone, the same PERIOD, and was corrected. ⛔ **Reverting that correction passes the round-trip arm with "decode exact" and fails the new one** — which is the whole point: our decoder sums high and low into a period and cannot see the split, so no encode-decode test in this file could ever have noticed. `counter_top / 2` is the obvious thing to write and it is exactly what was there before. ✅ The arm asserts every entry has `counter_top` 8 or 10 and a duty of 4, and reports **378 short + 165 long entries** on the bench frame. ⭐ **The failure line reports what it FOUND, not what it wanted** — "mark reaches 5, want 4" rather than restating the intention — because a red line that describes the test rather than the defect is one a reader skims | 1 arm, 1 deliberate break | ✓ **the break is the control and it separates the two arms cleanly**: round trip ✓, duty pin ⛔ | ✓ the expected shape comes from a real Flipper emission decoded byte-exact by our own reader (C226), not from our emitter | L201 |
| C238 | ⚠⚠ **NO SHAPE PIN IS POSSIBLE FOR GPROXII, AND THE REASON IS THE MEASUREMENT ITSELF — recorded as a negative rather than dressed up.** C237's AWID pin worked because a real emission's mark and gap could be measured separately. The same attempt for GProxII: a Flipper GProxII emission (captured with the CORRECT frame this time, 3 of 3 exact) and a real GProxII T5577 both slice to **HIGH runs of ~29 and LOW runs of ~36, ~67 and ~99** — a short fixed mark with a variable gap, which looks nothing like the symmetric held level our emitter describes. ⛔ **But that is not evidence our emitter is wrong**, because a HELD level through this front end produces exactly that pattern: the AC coupling measured at a ~27-sample time constant (C204) decays a 64-sample held level into a short high run followed by a long low one. The two hypotheses predict the same slicer output and the measurement cannot separate them. ⇒ **a pin built on it would encode an artefact of the receiver as a requirement on the transmitter.** ⭐ What the captures DO establish: a real tag and the Flipper's emulation are indistinguishable at this level, which is worth having and is why they are committed | 2 emissions compared, 3 reference captures | ✓ the reference captures decode 3 of 3 exact, so the recording is sound | ⚠ the negative is the finding — there is no control that would make this discriminating, and inventing one would be the mistake | L202 |
| C239 | ⭐⭐ **OUR AWID EMITTER NOW MATCHES THE FLIPPER'S OWN EMISSION DUTY EXACTLY — AND THE FLIPPER STILL WILL NOT READ IT, WHICH KILLS DUTY AS A CAUSE HARDER THAN C226 DID.** Measured on the committed real-tag captures, sliced the same way: a **real AWID T5577** runs HIGH 3 (1144 runs) or 4 (349) and LOW 5 (1270) or 6 (352) — so short tone ≈3+5 and long ≈4+6, roughly 37% and 40% duty. The **Flipper's own AWID emission** (C226) runs HIGH fixed at 4 and LOW 4 or 6 — 50% and 40%. **They differ**, and our emitter matches the FLIPPER: 4/4 and 4/6. ⇒ we now emit what the Flipper itself emits, and it reads us 0 of 6 while reading a real tag and its own emulation. ⚠ **The C141 CAVEAT APPLIES AND IS NOT WAVED AWAY**: slicer run lengths carry a systematic duty bias — 1-bit runs measuring 30.0 where the true period is 32.000 — so 3/5 may be a true 4/4 seen through that bias. But both emissions were measured through the SAME slicer, so the DIFFERENCE between them survives the caveat even if the absolute values do not. ⭐ Also measured, and reassuring: **Noralsy HIGH 16 / LOW 16 and Securakey HIGH 19 / LOW 21 are symmetric**, which is exactly what those emitters produce and they read 6/6 | 4 real-tag capture sets, one slicer | ✓ Noralsy and Securakey are the positive controls — symmetric tags, symmetric emitters, arms that work | ⚠ no control separates absolute duty from slicer bias, and the claim is limited to the difference | L203 |
| C240 | ✅ **THE EMULATION ENGINE IS ELIMINATED — it treats all three tag types identically and reports the two silent ones as transmitting.** `hw emudebug` read while each is emulating, one slot, one session: **AWID (307)** sense ENABLE, base clock **125kHz**, have pwm seq **True**, playbacks started **210**, frames per burst **14**; **GProxII (308)** the same with 11 frames; **Gallagher (304)**, the control that reads 6/6, the same with 21. ⭐ **The burst arithmetic is right for all three**: 14 × 37.6ms, 11 × 49ms and 21 × 24.6ms are all ≈520ms, so `recompute_frames_per_burst` is handling the variable-length FSK sequence and the biphase one correctly. ✅ The base clock is 125kHz for each, not the PSK1 path's 1MHz — the one thing `IS_PSK1_TYPE` could have got wrong. ⇒ **the sequence is loaded, the clock is right, the burst length is right and the peripheral is being told to play 210 times — and the Flipper hears one of the three.** Seventh hypothesis down, after counter_top magnitude, entries per bit, AC coupling, duty shape, the design itself and the buffer plumbing | 3 tag types, one slot, one session | ✓ **Gallagher is the control and its numbers are the same shape as the two that fail** — if the engine were the difference it would show here | ✓ the instrumentation reads the engine's own counters, not a behavioural inference | L204 |
| C241 | ✅ **ALL FIVE WORKING EMULATE ARMS ARE REGRESSION-CLEAN ON THE CURRENT BUILD, each with its own built-in control.** Worth running rather than assumed: since those arms were graded the shared sweep function has been RENAMED, `lf_sampled_read_phases` has changed signature four times (phase list, try count, drive, inter-capture gap), two tag types have been added, a whole biphase decoder has landed and Chameleon #1 has been reflashed a dozen times. **Keri PSK 4/4** (ASK arm 0/4), **NexWatch PSK 4/4** (ASK 0/4), **Securakey ASK 4/4** (PSK 0/4), **Noralsy ASK 4/4** (PSK 0/4), and **Gallagher ASK 6/6** (PSK 0/6), the latter run repeatedly this session as the control for the silent emitters. ⭐ **The opposite-modulation arm IS the control in each case** — it says the reader was looking and found a tag of THAT type, not any tag at all (C83) — and it came back empty every time. ⚠ These remain Flipper-verified only; C179's standing limitation is unchanged and the upgrade needs the tag lift | 22 Flipper reads across 5 protocols | ✓ every arm carries its own opposite-modulation null | ✓ the frames are the same credentials the grid records, so a pass here is comparable to the original grade | L205 |
| C242 | ⭐⭐⭐ **A HELD-LEVEL PWM ENTRY EMITS NOTHING — PROVED ON A WORKING, SHIPPED EMITTER BY CHANGING TWO ENTRIES, AND RESTORED.** The isolating control first: **em410x reads 4 of 4** at `counter_top` **64** with every entry a 50% square — so counter_top 64 is fine and it is not the size that matters. Then the A/B/A: making entries 20 and 21 of em410x's 64 emit a HELD level instead (`channel_0` 0 or 64 rather than 32) takes it to **0 of 4**, and restoring them brings it back to **4 of 4**. ⭐⭐ **That explains GProxII completely.** Its 0-bits ARE held levels — duty 0 or duty == counter_top for a whole bit period — so roughly half its frame emits silence and there is nothing for any reader to find. Both versions of the emitter had this, v1 as two held half-bits and v2 as one held bit, which is why re-shaping it changed nothing (C221, C222, C223). ⛔ **And it means BIPHASE CANNOT BE EMULATED THIS WAY AT ALL**: a biphase 0 is by definition a level held across the whole bit, and this peripheral does not transmit one. ⚠ **It does NOT explain AWID**, whose entries are all 50% squares — that failure is still open, and this finding must not be stretched over it | 1 shipped emitter, 2 entries changed, 12 Flipper reads | ✓ **A/B/A, not A/B** (M35): 4/4 working, 0/4 broken, 4/4 restored | ✓ em410x at counter_top 64 with all squares is the isolating control that separates size from shape | L206 |
| C243 | ⛔ **THE TONE VALUE IS NOT AWID'S PROBLEM EITHER — 8 and 12 is as silent as 8 and 10.** The last cheap probe available: the Flipper's AWID demodulator accepts any period in **[44, 100)** and calls it long at or above **72**, so 8 and 12 carrier cycles (64 and 96us) straddle that threshold just as 8 and 10 do, and the pulse counts (6 short per 0, 5 long per 1) are unchanged — a valid AWID emission by that demodulator's own rules, differing from ours only in the long tone's `counter_top`. **0 of 4.** ⇒ if a specific counter_top value were being mishandled, changing it would have shown. ⭐ **Ninth hypothesis down**: counter_top magnitude, entries per bit, AC coupling, duty shape, the design itself, the buffer plumbing, the emulation engine, held levels, and now the tone value. ✅ Restored, and the restore is checked two ways: `make check` green from a clean rebuild, and Gallagher 4 of 4 on the same slot afterwards | 4 Flipper reads at the probe value | ✓ **Gallagher 4/4 after the restore is the control** — the rig, the flash and the emulation path all still work | ✓ the probe value was chosen from Momentum's own window constants, not guessed | L207 |
| C244 | ✅ **C242 SURVIVES A CHALLENGE TO ITS OWN METHOD — held levels are silent whichever idiom expresses them — and a real bug in our GProxII emitter came out of asking.** ⚠ **The challenge**: `jablotron.c` and `pac.c` both hold a level and both use compare = **counter_top + 1**, each citing the nRF52840 PS — "compare >= counter_top -> pin held HIGH" — and warning that exactly counter_top leaves a **1-tick glitch** because the counter does reach it. Our GProxII emitter used `counter_top`, and so did C242's em410x probe. ⇒ what that probe demonstrated was that the GLITCHY form is silent, which is a weaker claim than the one it was written up as. ✅ **Re-run with compare = counter_top + 1: em410x still reads 0 of 4 with two held entries, and 4 of 4 restored.** So the finding holds and the mechanism claim is now earned rather than assumed. ✅ **GProxII's emitter is fixed regardless** — it was carrying a documented glitch on every held-high bit, which is a defect whether or not it was THE defect — and it still reads 0 of 6, as C242 predicts | 2 probes × 2 idioms, 14 Flipper reads | ✓ **A/B/A again**: 4/4 working, 0/4 with the correct-idiom held entries, 4/4 restored | ✓ the correct idiom came from two shipped emitters in this tree that use it and work | L209 |

What is confirmed is that Momentum registers both HID protocols with `LFRFIDFeatureASK`, the same feature as AWID, so `rfid read ask` does try them | 6 + 4 Flipper reads, one slot, one session | ✓ **Gallagher 4/4 on the same slot is the control** and it passed immediately after | ✓ the shipped emitter is the reference implementation — it is not ours and we did not write it | L210 |

With C245 that makes **HID Prox 0/6, ioProx 0/6, AWID 0/6**, against **Gallagher 4/4** run as the control immediately after each. ⭐ **The pattern is by MODULATION, not by authorship**: PSK1 (Keri, NexWatch) and ASK/Manchester (Gallagher, Securakey, Noralsy, em410x) all emulate; FSK2a does not, for anyone. ⛔ **So the grid's ✓ for HID Prox and ioProx EMULATE was inherited from upstream's feature list and has never been true on this bench** — corrected. ⚠ The same limit as C245 applies: no real HID or ioProx tag can be put in front of the Flipper from here, so its read path for those protocols is not independently confirmed — but three different FSK2a emitters failing while six non-FSK2a ones succeed is not a reader coincidence | 16 Flipper reads across 3 FSK2a protocols + 3 controls | ✓ **Gallagher 4/4 ran after every one of the three** | ✓ two of the three emitters are upstream code this project did not write | L211 |
| C247 | ⛔ **VARYING `counter_top` PER ENTRY IS NOT THE CAUSE — em410x with an alternating 63/65 still reads 4 of 4.** The hypothesis was worth testing because it is the one property that separates the FSK2a emitters from every working one: ASK and PSK1 sequences hold `counter_top` CONSTANT and vary only the polarity or the duty, while FSK2a alternates it between 8 and 10 every few entries. ✅ Probe: em410x's 64 entries given tops of 63 and 65 alternately, with the duty following — a ±1 tick jitter, 1.5% of a 512us bit and far inside any Manchester tolerance. **4 of 4 before, 4 of 4 with the jitter, 4 of 4 restored.** ⇒ the peripheral changes COUNTERTOP between periods without trouble. ⭐ **Tenth hypothesis down.** ⚠ **What that leaves cannot be separated on this bench, and saying so is the honest end of this line**: every working emitter uses a `counter_top` of 31 or more and every failing one uses 8 or 10 — but for a Manchester emitter a small top and a fast square wave are the SAME change, so "the magnitude" and "the resulting waveform" cannot be told apart by any probe available here | 3 probe builds, 12 Flipper reads | ✓ **A/B/A**: 4/4, 4/4 jittered, 4/4 restored | ✓ the jitter was sized from Momentum's own Manchester tolerance, not guessed | L212 |

C45 measured 22/24 (≈8%) on the white coin at rig A. ⇒ **The shared engine does not INHERIT the defect**, which is what U8's deferral was afraid of. ⛔ **But it is not shown to have FIXED it either, and the reason is worth stating rather than burying: the baseline defect is not present in this configuration, so there is nothing here for the new engine to have fixed.** Two candidate explanations survive and this measurement cannot separate them — (a) the **C47 advertising guard**, which `cf745fb` added to `lf_hidprox_data.c` and which is the ONLY change that decoder has had on this branch, and (b) the **package/coupling factor of C46**, where three tags with byte-identical memory read 0/6, 3/6 and 7/9. Separating them needs the white coin on a pad the Chameleon can reach, or a deliberate break of the guard | 80 device reads scored on the credential, not on pass/fail (M33), + 24 null reads | ✓ **A/B/A** (M35) — the closing HID arm rules out bench drift across the run | ✓ four blank columns, all silent: AWID reader on the HID tag **0/8**, HID reader on the AWID tag **0/8**, and both readers on the wiped tag **0/4** each | L214 |
| C249 | ⚠ **CHAINING `-c` ARGUMENTS IN ONE `pm3` INVOCATION CAN REPORT THE STATE BEFORE THE WRITE.** `./pm3 -c "lf t55xx wipe" -c "lf search"` printed the FULL pre-wipe HID credential — parity ok, raw and all — from a tag that had just been zeroed; the identical `lf search` in its own invocation a moment later returned `No known 125/134 kHz tags found!`. The wipe had landed. ⛔ Taken at face value that output is a clean, confident, **false** verification of a write that did not happen, in exactly the place this campaign puts its wipe-and-confirm-null step. ⇒ **Run the verifying read as its own `pm3` invocation, always** | 2 invocations, same tag, ~20 s apart | ✓ the separate-invocation read is the control and it disagrees | ✓ the subsequent AWID clone verified normally, so the tag and the link were both healthy throughout | L214 |
| C250 | ⭐⭐⭐ **C45 IS EXPLAINED, AND THE DEFECT IS WORSE THAN "INTERMITTENT": AN UNGUARDED HID READ RETURNS WRONG CREDENTIALS AS SUCCESSES.** The paired test `LF_ADV_GUARD_ENABLED` was put there for, run as A/B/A on one tag (H10301 FC 123 / CN 4567) in one session, each arm flashed from its own commit so `hw version`'s hash proves which build produced which number: **guard ON `768cdab`/`ee59b45` — 96 reads, 96 exact, 0 wrong, 0 null**; **guard OFF `d37b450` — 80 reads, 71 exact, 5 WRONG, 4 null** (11.2% defective, Fisher exact **p = 6.4e-4**). ⛔ **Five of the nine failures are not failures.** Of the two whose values were recorded, one returned FC 123 / **CN 4375** — bits 6 and 7 of the card number cleared, two adjacent bits inside ONE parity group, which H10301's parity cannot see — and one returned **FC 251** / CN 4567, a single bit set. That is the signature C47 predicted: a ~1.6 ms field collapse mangles a few bit-times and leaves the rest of the frame intact. ⛔ **I first wrote here that our read path checks no parity — that is FALSE and is retracted the same session it was published.** `unpack_h10301()` in `wiegand.c` checks both parity bits and the reader reaches it through `unpack()`; the grep that said otherwise only looked in `hidprox.c` and `lf_hidprox_data.c`, where the check does not live. ⇒ **The corruptions got through a parity check that was working, and HOW is not yet separated** (C251): either two flips inside one parity group, which is invisible by construction, or acceptance under a DIFFERENT 26-bit format — `lf hid prox read` passes `format_hint = 0`, and `unpack()` then tries every 26-bit format and returns the first one that fits. ⇒ C45's headline was wrong about the location: the fault is in the CAPTURE, perturbed by a BLE burst, not in the decode | 176 device reads across 3 flashed builds, credential-scored (M33) | ✓ **A/B/A** (M35) — 48/48 before, 48/48 after, and the restore arm is a different commit from the opening one | ✓ the guard-OFF build was verified on the device by git hash, not assumed from a flash that reported success (L162's lesson) | L215 |
| C251 | ⭐⭐⭐ **A CORRUPTED HID FRAME IS RELABELLED AS INDALA AND PRINTED AS A CONFIDENT CREDENTIAL — and that path, not parity blindness, is most of the damage.** Probe build `235dfa3`, guard OFF, one tag, two arms of 48: **`format_hint = 0`** (what `lf hid prox read` sends when you do not name a format) → 40 exact, **7 wrong**, 1 null; **`-f H10301`** → 46 exact, **1 wrong**, 1 null. ⛔ **Six of the seven came back as `Indala 26-bit`**, FC 1953-1977 / CN 471, clustered on **FC 1969 / CN 471** — which is precisely what the Proxmark reports as the ind26 reading of this tag's UNCORRUPTED frame. ⇒ The mechanism is now measured end to end: a burst flips a few bits, H10301's parity REJECTS the frame correctly, `unpack()` walks on to the next 26-bit format, ind26's weaker checks accept it, and the operator is shown an Indala credential for an HID tag with nothing marking it as a fallback. ⭐ **Pinning the format cuts wrong credentials 7/48 → 1/48.** ⭐ The four that DID come back as H10301 are all two flips inside ONE parity group, where the check is blind by construction: CN 4567→4375 (CN bits 6,7) and 4567→4823 (bits 8,9) in the odd group over CN[0:11]; FC 123→83 (FC bits 3,5) in the even group over CN[12:15]+FC; and FC 123→251, a lone FC bit 7, which fits only if the even-parity bit flipped with it — recorded as the inference it is. ⛔ **This is upstream's behaviour, not this branch's**: both the walking `unpack()` and the CLI's `format = 0` default are on `main` | 144 reads across 2 arms + a 48-read closing control, credential AND format scored | ✓ **A/B/A** (M35) — the guard restored (`a049bc6`) reads 48/48 clean on the same tag | ✓ the pinned arm is the discriminator: it disables the relabelling path and only the parity-blind corruptions survive it | L216 |
| C252 | ⭐⭐⭐ **EVERY FRAME GATE WE SHIP, MEASURED AGAINST A WRONG FRAME — and the spread is 100% to 12%.** C251 is about a decoder accepting a frame it should have refused, and nothing in this harness asked that question: every arm tested a CORRECT frame surviving a round trip. New `sweep()` in `ctest/roundtrip.c` flips one frame bit, re-emits through the shipping emitter and re-decodes through the shipping decoder, for all 96/64/224 bits of ten protocols. Rejected / accepted-wrong: **AWID 96/0 (100%)**, Gallagher 88/8, NexWatch 80/16, Noralsy 80/16, Indala26 33/31, Keri 33/31, IDTECK 32/32, **GProxII 36/60 (37%)**, **Securakey 19/77 (19%)**, **Indala224 28/196 (12%)**. ⭐ The extremes are explained by the formats, not by luck: AWID carries 22 INDEPENDENT odd-parity nibbles over bits 8..95, so any single flip breaks exactly one of them; Indala26 and IDTECK have a preamble and nothing else. ⛔⛔ **One number is ours to fix rather than to report: `LF_ASK_FORMAT_SECURAKEY.accept` is `NULL`**, where both its ASK siblings have a structural hook — and the reference implementation checks 9-bit `ParityAlways0` spacers across the frame that we do not. ⚠ **AND IT UNDERSTATES ANY FORMAT GATED ON REPETITION — see C254**, which also records why the obvious fix fails. ⚠ **What this does NOT measure**: the device readers require two agreeing stacks before reporting, which catches an error that does not repeat; these arms feed one perfect rendering of a corrupted frame, so they characterise the FRAME GATE alone | 1,024 corrupted frames through emitter and decoder, 10 protocols | ✓ **sensitivity proven by deliberate break** — `LF_ASK_FORMAT_GALLAGHER.accept` set to NULL moved it 88/8 → 16/80 and failed the harness, while its round-trip arm stayed ✓ exact, which is precisely the blindness this closes | ✓ counts are PINNED, so a gate that quietly weakens fails `make check` rather than printing a smaller number | L217 |
| C253 | ⭐⭐⭐ **SECURAKEY'S MISSING GATE IS CLOSED, AND IT BITES ON REAL AIR — 8/8 on a valid frame, 0/4 on one whose spacer is wrong.** C252 put Securakey second-weakest of everything we ship because `LF_ASK_FORMAT_SECURAKEY.accept` was `NULL`. The source note explaining that was wrong in its reasoning — it argued from "no checksum", and the reference's gate is not a checksum: `protocol_securakey_can_be_decoded` rejects any frame whose 9-bit groups do not open with a ZERO SPACER, ten of them at bits 10, 19, 28 … 91. `securakey_accept()` now enforces exactly those and nothing more. **On hardware, A/B/A on one tag:** the bench frame `7fcb400001adea5344300000` reads **8/8 exact**; the same frame with bit 46 — a spacer — set to 1, written and read back by the Proxmark so the tag provably holds it, reads **0 of 4, all silent**, while `lf sniff` shows the field fully modulated (peak-to-peak 16380, 6067 real field drops), so the tag is answering and it is the GATE refusing; restoring the valid frame returns **4/4 exact**. ⭐ Cost on the sweep: rejected 19→28, wrong 77→68. ⚠ **The frame also carries Wiegand parity at bits 35 and 63 that NEITHER reference checks on decode.** Enforcing it would make us stricter than both references on the strength of one bench credential, which is how a format starts refusing real tags — written down, left unenforced | 12 device reads across 3 tag states + 4 committed captures + 53 null captures + 96 swept frames | ✓ **A/B/A** (M35), and the broken arm is a DELIBERATE BREAK of one named bit, not a vague degradation | ✓ the tag was wiped first and the null confirmed on both the Proxmark and our reader (0/4) before anything was written | L218 |
| C254 | ⛔ **C252'S TABLE CANNOT REACH `require_repeat`, AND THE OBVIOUS WAY TO FIX THAT DOES NOT WORK — recorded so nobody spends the hour again.** Both renderers loop the sequence with `i % entries`, so the sweep emits a corrupted frame IDENTICALLY in every repetition and a format whose gate is "the same frame twice" sees two agreeing copies of the corruption. Indala224's 12% is partly that artefact. ✅ **The fix attempted**: render one corrupted pass, then clean passes — what a burst actually does. **It failed on its own control.** Indala26, which has no `require_repeat` and should therefore not move, went 33/0/31 → **0 rejected, 39 silent, 25 wrong**: once the buffer contains a VALID frame the decoder simply finds it, so "rejected" becomes nearly unreachable and the split between silent and wrong is decided by which copy comes FIRST. ⛔ And Indala224 did not move at all (28/0/196, identical) for a second reason: flipping one bit of an even-parity frame makes it odd, the differential emitter then DOUBLES its buffer, and "one pass" is already two corrupted copies. ⇒ **What the instrument would need**: a buffer capped at exactly two passes, corrupted first, with the corruption chosen to leave the frame's parity — and so the emitter's doubling — unchanged. ⚠ Until then the table's low numbers are a FLOOR for repeat-gated formats, not an estimate | 2 sweeps × 288 corrupted frames, one of them the control that killed it | ✓ the control is what settled it — a test arm whose control moves as far as the test measures the splice, not the gate | ✓ the parity-doubling explanation is read off the emitter's own `want_entries` rule, which the round-trip arm already asserts | L219 |

| C267 | ⭐⭐⭐ **A VALID TAG AT MARGINAL FIELD PRODUCES WRONG CREDENTIALS TOO — 4 in 14 frames — AND THE TWO-AGREEING-STACKS RULE IS WHAT STOPS THEM, NOT THE FRAME GATES.** C266's false frames needed a deliberately parity-broken tag, which leaves open whether ordinary marginality does the same. It does, and worse. The tag holds the CORRECT GProxII credential throughout; `rdrcap.py` captures through the reader's own path at the reader's own six phases, decoded by the shipping decoder: **drive 7 — 6 captures, 6 frames, 6 exact, 0 false**; **drive 6 — 18 captures, 12 frames, 4 of them not the true raw**; **drive 4 — 18 captures, 0 frames at all**; **drive 2 — 18 captures, 2 frames, neither the true raw**. ⛔ **Scored on the CREDENTIAL rather than the raw frame — which is what a user acts on — 4 of the 14 frames are WRONG**: FC 44/card 7044, FC 45/card 6861, FC 40/card 6789 and FC 12/card 21124, against the true FC 45 / card 6789. ⚠ **The other two "false" raws decode to the CORRECT credential** — they differ only in bits outside the credential field, so classifying by raw hex OVER-COUNTS and saying so matters. ⭐⭐ **All four wrong credentials are DISTINCT, so the reader's two-agreeing-stacks rule would have rejected every one.** That is a measured argument for the agreement rule and against leaning on the frame gates: C252's table cannot see this at all. ⚠ **And the contrast with C266 is the sharp part** — on the BROKEN tag, false frames survived that same rule 3 times in 52 device reads, so they repeated. ⇒ Marginal field: caught by agreement. Broken frame: not always | 60 captures through the reader's own path at 4 drives × 6 phases, decoded by the shipping decoder; 8 committed under `caps/gproxii-marginal/` | ✓ drive 7 is the control and it is 6/6 exact in the same minutes | ✓ every false frame re-run through the full gate offline — spacers, length and Wiegand parity all pass, so they are not a hole in the reader | L231 |
| C268 | ⭐⭐⭐ **NOT ONE MARGINAL-FIELD ERROR REPEATED IN 92 CAPTURES — so "two agreeing stacks would have caught them" stops being an inference and becomes a measurement.** C267 argued the agreement rule saves this reader because its four wrong credentials were distinct; four is not enough to rest a defence on. Re-captured 8 times at each of the four drive-6 phases that had produced an odd frame — 32 more captures, same valid tag, same path. **19 frames, and every non-true frame appeared EXACTLY ONCE**: the true frame came back 5, 4, 0 and 4 times at the four phases while six odd frames came back once each. ⇒ Across both runs: **92 captures, 33 frames, 12 odd raws, and all 12 unique.** A marginal capture's error does not reproduce, so a rule demanding two agreeing captures rejects it — measured, not argued. ⛔ **9 of the 12 are WRONG CREDENTIALS** (FC 44/7044, 45/6861, 40/6789, 12/21124, 108/6789, 45/4749, 13/15012, 45/8069, 44/2693 against a true FC 45 / card 6789), so what the agreement rule is catching is not cosmetic. ⭐ **And the pattern differs from C266's**: the broken tag's false frames ALL kept card 6789 because they sat one bit from a frame whose card field was intact, while marginal-field errors scatter across both fields. Two different failure modes, and only one of them repeats | 92 captures at 4 drives × 6 phases plus 4 phases × 8 repeats, all through the reader's own path | ✓ the true frame repeating 13 times across the same captures is the positive control — the pipeline CAN produce a repeat, so "none repeated" is not an artefact of never seeing one | ✓ each odd frame re-run through the full gate offline and each passes on its own merits | L232 |
| C269 | ⭐⭐⭐ **WHY C266'S FALSE FRAMES SURVIVE AGREEMENT AND C268'S DO NOT: the broken tag's error population is SMALL AND CONCENTRATED, so two captures can land on the same wrong frame.** Captured the parity-broken tag through the reader's own path at its own drive and six phases, 8 repeats each — 48 captures, the same method C268 used on the valid tag, so the two are finally comparable. ⭐ **The on-tag frame was returned 0 times in 48**: the Wiegand gate refuses it on every single capture, which is the gate working exactly as C255 built it. **13 frames, 12 distinct, and 12 of 12 are WRONG CREDENTIALS** — where the valid tag at marginal field gave 9 wrong of 12 (C268). ⛔ **11 of the 12 keep card 6789 and differ only in the facility code** (43, 63, 111, 175, 15 …), because the tag's frame already fails parity by one bit and only a small nudge re-balances it — so the nudges land in the same few places. ⭐⭐ **One frame recurred — `f84602a4611ad4a114211046`, FC 43 / card 6789 — at two DIFFERENT capture phases.** ⛔ **I read that as the shape that passes two-agreeing-stacks, and it is NOT — corrected by C293.** The rule requires two CONSECUTIVE decodes at the SAME phase and resets when the phase changes, so cross-phase recurrence never satisfies it. What does defeat it is deterministic distortion at one configuration, which our own `gproxii_read` comment has documented since C207. **Marginal field scatters its errors and agreement catches them all (C268: 12 odd frames, 0 recurrences); a broken frame concentrates them and agreement sometimes cannot.** ⚠ Both captures of the recurring frame decode at the decoder's internal phase 4 despite being taken at SAADC phases 104 and 120 — the alignment, not the capture phase, is what repeats | 48 captures + an 8-read A/B/A control on the restored tag; 3 committed under `caps/gproxii-broken/` | ✓ the restored tag reads 8/8 exact afterwards, so the bench did not drift | ✓ the two committed captures reproduce the recurring frame from two different phases, checkable without the bench | L233 |
| C270 | ⭐⭐⭐ **THE FALSE-CREDENTIAL PROBLEM IS A PHYSICAL-LAYER PROPERTY OF RF/64, NOT A WEAK GATE — and the protocol with the STRONGEST gate is the only one that has it.** Swept field strength on all three ASK-family protocols through the reader's own capture path, same tag position, same session: **Gallagher (RF/32, half-bit 16 samples)** — drives 7/6/4/2 all exact, **0 false in 40 captures**, then a cliff to 0 frames at drive 1; **Securakey (RF/40, 20 samples)** — identical shape, **0 false in 40 captures**, cliff at drive 1; **GProxII (RF/64, 32 samples)** — degrades GRADUALLY into wrong answers, 12 odd raws and 9 wrong credentials in 92 captures (C267, C268). ⭐ **The front end's AC coupling has a ~27-sample time constant (C204).** RF/32 and RF/40 put their half-bit well inside it, so the level is either cleanly sliceable or gone — perfect, then nothing. RF/64 puts it OUTSIDE, which is why GProxII has a degraded regime at all and why that regime produces frames rather than silence. ⛔⛔ **This inverts the natural reading of C252's table.** GProxII's gate is the STRONGEST of the three — preamble, 18 spacers, format length and Wiegand parity — and Securakey's is the weakest, spacers only; yet GProxII produces wrong credentials and Securakey produces none. **Gate strength and field robustness are independent, and C252 measures only the first.** ⚠ Zero false in 40 captures is good evidence, not a zero rate; Gallagher and Securakey were swept at 4 phases × 2 repeats against GProxII's 92. ⚠ FDX-B is RF/32 and should sit in the safe class — predicted, not measured | 120 captures across 3 protocols × 5 drives, all through `lf_reader_capture_probe` | ✓ each protocol's own known-good credential, cloned by the Proxmark, with drive 7 as the in-sweep control every time | ✓ the cliff at drive 1 reproduces on both ASK protocols and on two separate passes, so "no marginal regime" is a measurement rather than an absence of looking | L234 |
| C271 | ⚠ **`rdrcap.py --drive 0` SILENTLY MEANS DRIVE 4.** `cmd_processor_lf_reader_capture` reads it as `(data[2] != 0) ? data[2] : 4`, so a zero is the default rather than a floor — and `lf_125khz_radio_drive_set()`'s own clamp (`<1` → 1) never sees it. It cost twenty minutes: drive 0 read a tag 8 of 8 where drive 1 read 0 of 8, which looks exactly like C148's inert-drive defect striking mid-session and was chased as such. ⇒ The tool's `--drive` help should say so, and any drive sweep should start at 1 rather than 0 | 3 sweeps at drive 0 and drive 1, reproducing both ways | ✓ settled by reading the handler, not by inferring from behaviour — the behaviour is what made it look like a hardware fault | ✓ C148 is EXCLUDED rather than assumed away: drive 1 gave 0-of-4 twice more after the drive-0 result, so the control was live throughout | L234 |
| C272 | ⭐⭐⭐ **FDX-B SETTLES C270'S HYPOTHESIS THE HARD WAY: SAME BIPHASE DECODER AS GPROXII, RF/32 INSTEAD OF RF/64, AND ZERO WRONG CREDENTIALS.** GProxII and FDX-B share `lf_ask_biphase.c` whole — same decoder, same acceptance machinery, same capture engine — and differ in bit rate and format. Swept the same way: **56 captures, 24 frames, and not one wrong credential.** ⇒ The false-credential behaviour cannot be the biphase decoder, because the decoder is identical; it is RF/64's half-bit exceeding the front end's ~27-sample AC-coupling constant, exactly as C270 proposed. ⭐ **One raw frame did differ, and it is the case C267 warned about**: `…58044201` against `…58040201`, a SINGLE bit at position 113 — past bit 64, in the tail the CRC-16 does not cover — and country 999 / national 1234567890 / animal all decode identically. A wrong raw is not a wrong credential, and scoring by hex would have logged a false frame that does not exist. ⚠ The unprotected tail is the reader's own printed warning (`the CRC covers the first 8 bytes only`), and this is the first time it has been seen to matter | 56 captures across 5 drives × 4 phases; 2 committed under `caps/fdxb-drive/` | ✓ the discriminator is the SHARED DECODER — Gallagher and Securakey use a different one, so C270 alone could not separate decoder from bit rate and this does | ✓ the differing bit was located to position 113 and the credential recomputed from both raws, not assumed equal | L235 |
| C273 | ⛔ **FDX-B READS AT DRIVE 4 AND AT NO OTHER FIELD STRENGTH — 16 of 16 at drive 4, 0 of 16 at drive 7, 0 of 16 at drive 6, 0 of 8 at drives 2 and 1.** Reproduced across two independent passes. ⭐ It works today only because the shared ASK reader sweeps `{4, 7, 6, 2}` **with 4 first**, which is why C214 read it 6/6 without anyone noticing how narrow the window is. ⛔ **That ordering is load-bearing and nothing says so in the code**: reorder the sweep, shorten it, or pin a drive the way `gproxii_read()` pins 7, and FDX-B stops reading with no other symptom. ⚠ C182 established the opposite failure for Noralsy — it decodes at drive 7 and at no other — so the sweep is carrying two protocols that need DIFFERENT single settings, and neither is documented at its call site | 56 captures over 5 drives, drive 7 and 6 re-run in a second pass to rule out a fluke | ✓ drive 4 giving 16/16 in the same sweeps is the positive control — the tag and the path are fine, it is the field setting that decides | ✓ two passes hours apart in the same session, same tag position | L235 |
| C274 | ⛔ **A COMMENT IN SHIPPING CODE ASSERTED A SAFETY PROPERTY THE SWEEP DOES NOT HAVE — corrected, and nothing is broken today only because of a 3× margin nobody had noticed was load-bearing.** `LF_ASK_DRIVE_STEPS` said *"step order matters for latency, not correctness"*. C182 has Noralsy reading at drive 7 and no other; C273 has FDX-B reading at drive 4 and no other. For those two the sweep is not an optimisation, it is the only reason they read. ⛔ **And the table TRUNCATES**: `steps = timeout_ms / 250` takes a PREFIX, so a 250 ms budget reaches only drive 4 and 500 ms only {4,7} — Noralsy needs the second entry and would go unreadable with no other symptom. ⭐ **Traced to the actual numbers rather than left as a worry**: every caller passes `INDALA_READ_TIMEOUT_MS = 3000`, which asks for 12 steps and clamps to 4, so all four are always tried and **there is no live bug**. ⇒ The margin is the whole protection, and *shortening the timeout to make reads snappier* is the innocuous-looking change that breaks Noralsy. The note now says so at the table | 5 timeout values worked through against the step arithmetic; the firmware rebuilt clean | ✓ the claim is checked against the constant every caller actually passes, not against the host's 10 s CLI timeout, which is a different number and does not govern this | ✓ comment-only change — no behaviour to verify, and saying that is better than inventing a test for it | L236 |
| C275 | ⚠ **A SAMPLE PHASE CAN BE USEFUL AT FULL SIGNAL AND EMIT NOTHING BUT GARBAGE AT MARGINAL SIGNAL — so a phase map measured at one signal level cannot show which phases are dangerous.** Aggregated the GProxII captures by CAPTURE phase, the way the Indala 60-92 dead band was mapped. **At drive 7, the reader's own setting, all six phases return the true frame.** At drives 6 and 2, 14 captures each: phase 0 → 7 true / 2 odd, phase 112 → 6/2, phase 20 → 6/4, and **phase 120 → 0 TRUE, 3 odd, 11 silent — it never once produced the correct frame**. On the parity-broken tag at drive 7, phase 120 produced **6 of the 13 odd frames** from one of six phases. ⇒ Phase 120 is not a dead band at the operating point; it is **the first phase to fail as the field weakens, and the only one that yields exclusively wrong frames once it does**. ⛔ **This is why "drop it" is NOT the conclusion**: at drive 7 it is a contributing phase, and the reader runs at drive 7. ⇒ What it does establish is that `PHASE_ROTATION`'s own warning — *"one tag, one unit, two coupling geometries … it could move"* — understates the problem: the ranking is a function of SIGNAL LEVEL too, and every phase map in this tree was measured at one level | 56 valid-tag captures across 4 phases at 2 marginal drives + 48 broken-tag captures across 6 phases + 6 drive-7 controls | ✓ the drive-7 row is the control and it is TRUE at all six phases, so "phase 120 is bad" is false as stated and the finding had to be narrowed | ⚠ **the drive-7 control is one capture per phase** — enough to refute "dead band", not enough to rank phases at the operating point | L237 |
| C276 | ⭐⭐ **THE UNPINNED HID READ NOW SAYS IT IS A GUESS — and `unpack()` turns out to have EXACTLY ONE CALLER, which narrows the upstream conversation considerably.** C251 measured a corrupted H10301 frame coming back as a confident `Indala 26-bit` 6 times in 7, because `unpack()` returns the FIRST layout that fits and we print it as the answer — where the Proxmark prints EVERY match under a *"False Positives ARE possible"* banner. `lf hid prox read` now prints, when and only when no format was pinned: *"⚠ no format pinned — this is the FIRST layout that fits, not the only one"*, names the exact `-f` flag to require it, and quotes C251's 7-in-48 against 1-in-48. Verified on a real H10301 clone: unpinned warns, `-f H10301` is silent. ⭐⭐ **And §9f was too broad about the risk of changing the walk.** It said narrowing `unpack()` touches *"every reader that guesses a format"*. A whole-tree grep says there is **one caller: `hidprox.c:134`.** Nothing else in the firmware calls it — ioProx has a fixed XSF layout and Indala prints its own 26-bit interpretation without the table. ⇒ The upstream question is not "do not disturb the shared walk", it is a change confined to one reader — worth knowing before the conversation starts | 2 device reads on a cloned H10301, one per branch + a whole-tree caller grep | ✓ both branches exercised, not just the one that shows the new text | ✓ the caller count is a grep over `application/src`, excluding the `unpack_*` per-format helpers and the definition itself | L238 |
| C277 | ⭐⭐⭐ **A VALID H10301 FRAME IS ACCEPTED BY TWO FORMATS, AND A CORRUPTED ONE IS NEVER STILL H10301 — measured exhaustively against the shipping `wiegand.c`, not sampled.** New `ctest/ambig.c` compiles the real `wiegand.c` (Nordic's macros shimmed verbatim, `parity.h` included as-is) and asks `unpack()` one format at a time. **The bench credential FC 123 / CN 4567 is accepted by H10301 (FC 123, card 4567) AND by IND26 (FC 1969, card 471)** — the same two the Proxmark printed under its *"False Positives ARE possible"* banner at the start of this session. ⇒ **`unpack()`'s first-match ordering decides the answer on EVERY read, not only on corrupted ones**, and `lf hid prox read` reports H10301 solely because it precedes IND26 in the table. ⛔ **And of all 26 single-bit corruptions: 13 are rejected by every format, 13 are first-matched by a DIFFERENT format, and ZERO still match H10301.** That is why C251 saw 6-in-7 relabelled — relabelling is not an accident, it is what corruption does here. ⇒ **Narrowing the walk is therefore NOT cheap** (C276 wondered): reorder or truncate the table and working readers change what they report. ⭐ Counts are PINNED in `make check`, so a table edit says so | 1 valid frame + 26 corruptions × 42 formats, exhaustive | ✓ the shipping `wiegand.c` is compiled, not transcribed — the answer is the firmware's own | ✓ the two-match result independently reproduces the Proxmark's own output on the same credential | L239 |
| C278 | ⭐⭐⭐ **`lf hid clone -w ind26 --fc 1969 --cn 471` AND `-w H10301 --fc 123 --cn 4567` PRODUCE THE SAME TAG — the ambiguity is not a corruption artefact, it is the format table.** Wrote a genuine Indala-26 credential onto an HID Prox tag with the Proxmark; our reader returns **H10301 FC 123 / CN 4567**, the credential C248 has been using all session. Identical 26 bits, two valid readings, and which one you are told depends on which tool you ask. ⭐ **Swept in `ctest/ambig.c`: H10301 accepts ALL 8,658 validly packed ind26 frames tested.** ⇒ At 26 bits a non-HID format can NEVER win the walk, because H10301 precedes it and accepts everything it accepts — so a genuine Indala-layout tag reports as H10301 and the "no HID layout fits" path is reachable **only through corruption**, which is exactly what C251 saw. ⛔ **This caught a wrong message before it shipped, twice.** The first draft asserted a non-HID match means corruption; writing a real ind26 tag showed that a non-HID credential is a real thing, so I hedged it to "either" — and the sweep then showed the hedge is wrong AT 26 BITS, where it really is only corruption. The message is now length-aware: corruption for IND26, "the reader cannot tell" for longer formats where no HID layout need accept a valid foreign credential | 4 ind26 credentials written to a real tag + an 8,658-frame overlap sweep against the shipping `wiegand.c` | ✓ the same-tag result is two independent tools disagreeing about one physical frame, which is stronger than either tool's self-report | ⚠ **the corruption branch's text is NOT freshly verified on hardware** — reaching it needs the guard-off build, and C251's six observations are what say it fires | L240 |
| C279 | ⭐⭐ **C278'S RELABELLING MESSAGE HAS NOW BEEN SEEN TO RENDER — on the branch it was written for, which no test could reach without deliberately corrupting a capture.** C278 shipped user-facing text on a path C251 proved FIRES but that nobody had walked with the text in place; a wrong f-string there would have stayed wrong indefinitely. Flashed the guard-off probe (`512a7be`), read the H10301 tag 40 times, and **read 29 walked it**: `HIDProx/Indala 26-bit` followed by *"⛔ no HID layout fits this frame … At 26 bits that means the capture was corrupted … The numbers below are fiction — re-read"*, then `FC: 1973  CN: 471`. ⭐ **And the credential is C266's signature in a second protocol**: the uncorrupted frame's ind26 reading is FC 1969 / card 471, so the corruption moved the facility code by 4 and left the card number untouched — right card, wrong facility, exactly as the broken GProxII tag behaved. ⚠ **1 of 40, where C251 measured 7 wrong in 48 with 6 relabelled** — a different session and coupling, and not reconciled; the rate is reported, not claimed to match. ⭐ The restore also brings #2 to HEAD, closing the "one commit behind" note | 40 guard-off reads + 12 guard-on closing reads across 2 flashed builds | ✓ **A/B/A** (M35) — 12/12 correct on the restored build, so the bench did not drift | ✓ the branch was reached by the same instrument C250/C251 used, not by a mock or a unit test standing in for it | L241 |
| C280 | ⛔ **THE MESSAGE C278/C279 SHIPPED WOULD HAVE CRIED WOLF ON EVERY 27-, 28-, 29- AND 30-BIT READ — because there is NO HID FORMAT AT THOSE LENGTHS AT ALL.** Extended `ctest/ambig.c` to ask, per bit length, whether some HID format accepts every valid foreign frame there. **26, 32 and 37 bits: yes, totally** (121/121, 363/363, 121/121) — a foreign winner is necessarily corruption. **34 bits: partly** (307 of 484) — both explanations live, the reader cannot tell. ⛔ **27, 28, 29 and 30 bits: there is no HID format to accept anything.** `ind27`, `indasc27`, `tecom27`, `w2804`, `ind29` and `atsw30` are the ONLY layouts at those lengths, so a foreign result is simply what the reader found — and the `⛔ no HID layout fits this frame` text would have fired on every one of them, every time. ⇒ The message now branches three ways on measured ground: corruption at 26/32/37, "the reader cannot tell" at 34, and a neutral note where no HID format exists. ⚠ Lengths above 37 are NOT measured — the credential grid produces no valid frames there — and fall through to the cautious wording. ⭐ The per-length VERDICTS are pinned in `make check` (not the counts, which depend on the grid), because the CLI now branches on them | 8 bit lengths × every foreign format × a credential grid, against the shipping `wiegand.c` | ✓ caught by extending the measurement rather than by a user hitting it — the 27-30 case has no tag on this bench and would never have appeared | ✓ vendor labels taken from the table's own comments, so `MDI37 // PointGuard MDI` is foreign by the table's word and not by mine | L242 |
| C281 | ⭐ **THE AMBIGUITY SWEEP IS COMPLETE, NOT PARTIAL — there are no formats above 37 bits — and the unimplemented enum names are unreachable from the CLI, so the defect I went looking for does not exist.** C280 said lengths above 37 were "not measured". The real reason nothing appeared there: **`formats[]` has 31 entries and stops at 37 bits.** The firmware's `card_format_t` declares **42** names — AVIG56, BC40, BQT38, C1K48S, CASI40, DEFCON32, H800002, IR56, ISCS, P10001, PW39 have no table row — so `unpack()` can never return them and `pack()` returns 0 for them. ⇒ C280's per-length table covers every format the code can actually produce, and the CLI's cautious fall-through wording applies to nothing. ⛔ **The predicted defect was refused by the CLI**: `lf hid prox write -f C1K48S` errors with *invalid choice*, listing exactly 31 — because the HOST's `HIDFormat` enum has 31 members and mirrors the table, where the firmware's has 42. A silent zero-write is therefore not reachable, and the tag was untouched (both readers still H10301 FC 123 / CN 4567 afterwards). ⚠ What remains is dead surface a reviewer will trip over — 42 declared against 31 implemented, in a header that is upstream's ⚠ **And citing those names broke `checkdocs.sh`, which is its own small finding**: `refs()` matched `\bC[0-9]+` with no trailing boundary, so `C1K48S` read as a citation of claim C1 and was reported missing. The trailing `\b` is now there and its sensitivity re-proved with a deliberate `C999` | 1 rejected write + 3 device reads + 1 Proxmark read + an enum/table diff | ✓ the negative result is confirmed by the CLI's own error listing all 31 accepted names | ✓ the tag was verified unchanged by BOTH readers after the attempt | L243 |
| C282 | ⛔ **THE MESSAGE I ADDED IN C280 DROPPED THE ISSUE LEVEL AND OEM FIELDS — caught by re-reading what I shipped, not by any test, because no tag of those lengths exists on this bench.** The `no HID format at this length` branch printed its note and then `return`ed, re-printing FC and CN itself and skipping IL and OEM entirely; it also emitted a blank line when FC was 0. Restructured into a clean three-way branch that falls through to the shared field printing. ⭐ **And the classification is now verified structurally, which is the part hardware cannot reach**: the three sets are **disjoint**, and exactly four formats fall through to the "cannot tell" wording — `N10002`, `OPTUS34`, `SMP34`, `BQT34` — which are precisely the four non-HID 34-bit formats, the one length C280 measured as PARTIAL coverage (307 of 484). Every format the table implements lands in exactly one branch, and the branch each lands in matches its measured length. ⚠ **Three of the four branches remain unexercised on hardware** and will stay so: 27-30 and 34-bit tags do not exist here, and the corruption branch needs the guard-off build (C279 walked that one) | 2 device reads + a disjointness-and-coverage check against the real `HIDFormat` enum | ✓ the fall-through set matching C280's partial-coverage length exactly is an independent check on the classification — I did not build the sets from that | ✓ the regression was found by reading the shipped code, and is recorded rather than quietly fixed | L244 |
| C283 | ⭐⭐ **ALL FOUR BRANCHES OF THE HID MESSAGE ARE NOW EXERCISED ON HARDWARE — C282 said three of them never could be, and that was wrong.** The thing I had missed: the Proxmark WRITES those formats. `lf hid clone -w <fmt>` puts any layout in the shared table onto an HID Prox tag, so the branches are reachable with no corruption and no hands. **`-w ind27 --fc 100 --cn 200`** → *"note: no HID format exists at this frame length — Indala 27-bit is simply the layout that fits, and that is normal here"*, FC 100 / CN 200, **no ⛔ and no alarm** — which is C280's crying-wolf case shown not to cry. **`-w Optus34 --fc 1 --cn 1`**, a credential `ambig` named as taken by no HID format → *"⛔ no HID layout fits … at this length the reader cannot tell"*, FC 1 / CN 1. The hedge is correct there: this tag really does carry a foreign layout, which is case (a), and the reader says so rather than guessing. ⭐ With C279's corruption branch and the pinned/unpinned HID branches, that is 4 of 4. ⚠ Three N10002 credentials read back as **H10306** first — at 34 bits an HID format takes 307 of 484, so finding the branch needed `ambig` to name a frame in the other 177 rather than guessing one | 5 credentials written by the Proxmark and read back, + the full harness green | ✓ each branch's text checked against the credential that was cloned, not merely observed to appear | ✓ bench restored to H10301 FC 123 / CN 4567, 4 of 4 | L245 |
| C284 | ⭐⭐⭐ **HALF THE HID FORMAT TABLE CANNOT SURVIVE AN UNPINNED READ — 15 of the 29 formats written to a REAL tag come back as a DIFFERENT format with a DIFFERENT credential.** C277 measured the ambiguity for one format on the host; C283 showed the Proxmark can write any of them, so this asks the hardware for all of them. Every format the CLI accepts, cloned by `lf hid clone -w <fmt> --fc 1 --cn 1` and read back unpinned: **14 round-trip exactly** (H10301, IND27, IND29, ATSW30, ADT31, HCP32, D10202, H10306, OPTUS34, C1K35S, C15001, S12906, H10302, P10004) and **15 are relabelled** — IND26→H10301 (cn 4097), KASTLE→HCP32 (cn 8389632), KANTECH→HCP32 (cn 1024), WIE32→HCP32 (cn 512), HPP32→HCP32 (cn 4096), SMP34→Optus34 (cn 8), BQT34→H10306 (fc 256), N10002→H10306, SIE36→Simplex36 (cn 65537), H10320→H10302 (cn 21), H10304→H10302 (cn 524289), MDI37→H10302 (cn 536870913), HGEN37→P10004 (fc 3072), INDASC27→ind27 (cn 24), TECOM27→ind27 (fc 1152). ⛔ **The credential is wrong in every relabelled case, not just the name** — a Kastle tag holding fc 1 / cn 1 reads as Check Point card 8389632. ⭐ **And pinning recovers it exactly**: `-f KASTLE` → Kastle fc 1 cn 1, `-f IND26` → Indala 26-bit fc 1 cn 1, `-f SMP34` → Cardkey Smartpass fc 1 cn 1, 3 of 3. So the tags are perfect and the WALK is what loses the information. ⇒ C276's *"no format pinned"* warning is not a corner case; it covers half the table. ⚠ W2804 and ACTPHID are missing because the Proxmark refused every credential shape tried — a writer limitation, not a reader finding | 29 formats × (1 Proxmark clone + 1 unpinned read) + 3 pinned controls | ✓ the pinned control is what separates "the walk loses it" from "the tag is wrong" — without it this would only show disagreement | ✓ each credential is fc 1 / cn 1, chosen so the same value is legal in nearly every format and a relabel is obvious on sight | L246 |
| C285 | ⭐⭐⭐ **C284'S RELABELLING IS STRUCTURAL: 12 of the 31 `unpack_*` functions ACCEPT ANYTHING of the right bit length, so a first-match walk cannot be made correct.** Audited every unpacker for a rejection path: **19 have one** (parity, spacers or range) and **12 have none at all** — `ind27`, `indasc27`, `tecom27` (one shared `unpack_nonlinear` helper), `ind29`, `adt31`, `hcp32`, `hpp32`, `kantech`, `wie32`, `optus`, `smartpass`, `p10004`. ⇒ **That list IS C284's relabel map**: HCP32 swallows KASTLE, KANTECH, WIE32 and HPP32 at 32 bits; Optus34 swallows SMP34; ind27 swallows INDASC27 and TECOM27; P10004 swallows HGEN37. The walk lands on the first format that checks NOTHING. ⭐ **And the guarded ones did their job** — our reader returned Optus34 rather than H10306 for the SMP34 tag precisely because `unpack_h10306` verified parity and refused, which the Proxmark independently marks `parity ( fail )` on that frame. ⛔ **These cannot be hardened.** A format with no parity or checksum has nothing to check, and the reference agrees: `lf hid reader` prints a parity verdict beside H10306, N10002 and BQT34 and NONE beside HCP32 or Optus34. ⇒ **The walk is not narrowable into correctness (C277 asked); the only correct behaviours are the Proxmark's — print every candidate with its parity — or pinning with `-f`.** That is the conclusion to take upstream | 31 unpackers audited + 3 tags read by both tools, 5 candidates each (⛔ counts corrected from "13 of 32" by C300, which MEASURED them; the list and the conclusion are unchanged) | ✓ the check-less list predicts C284's relabel map exactly, which neither was derived from the other | ✓ the Proxmark's own parity column is independent confirmation that the check-less formats have nothing to check rather than checks we skipped | L247 |
| C286 | ⚠ **§9f HAD BECOME A RECORD OF HOW I GOT THERE, AND ITS TAIL ACTIVELY MISLED — rewritten.** The section a reviewer is handed had grown by accretion across C251→C285, with each new understanding appended below the last. Three statements in its tail were contradicted by claims above them: *"must not fix it here: the format walk is load-bearing for every reader that guesses a format"* (one caller, C276), *"what to report, not to patch"* (a mitigation shipped, C276), and — the damaging one — *"this is reachable only when something else is already perturbing the capture"*, which **C284 disproved on perfectly good tags**. A reviewer reading to the end would have concluded the defect only matters under corruption. ⇒ Rewritten to state what is true now, strongest measurement first: half the table fails an unpinned read on VALID tags, 13 check-less unpackers are why, narrowing cannot fix it, and corruption is a separate and smaller part of the story rather than the whole of it. Same length, and `LOG.md` keeps the history. ⭐ **The general lesson is the one NEXT.md's own convention already states and I was not following**: a planning document that only ever gets appended to becomes a stratigraphy, and the oldest layer is the one a reader reaches last | 1 section, 3 contradicted statements identified against the ledger | ✓ each contradiction is named with the claim that refutes it, so this is a demonstrable correction rather than a matter of taste | ✓ nothing was dropped — the corruption measurements are still there, ranked below the stronger result instead of standing in for it | L248 |
| C287 | ⭐⭐⭐ **`lf hid prox read` NOW NAMES THE OTHER LAYOUTS THAT FIT — the behaviour C285 identified as one of only two correct ones, built and verified against the Proxmark.** The firmware owns `formats[]`, so it does the enumerating: new `wiegand_other_matches()` returns how many other layouts of the same bit length accept the frame and names up to two, reported in **payload bytes 13-15, which were already present and already zero** — so an older host reading `[:13]` is unaffected and a newer host on older firmware reads "no other match", which is the honest degradation. ⛔ A host-side copy of 31 unpackers was rejected as exactly the "share the front end, do not resemble it" mistake; the host only maps ids to names. **Verified against the reference on three tags**: Kastle → Check Point + **4 others** against pm3's 5 candidates; SMP34 → Optus34 + **1** against pm3's 5, of which 3 are annotated `parity ( fail )` and our unpackers correctly refuse; H10304 → H10302 + **3** against pm3's 5, one of which fails parity. ⇒ **Our counts equal the Proxmark's parity-PASSING candidates exactly, 3 for 3** — we report the genuine alternatives and omit the ones the reference itself marks as failing. ⭐ On the bench H10301 tag it reports exactly 1 other, `Indala 26-bit`, which is C277's two-format result appearing at the CLI. ⚠ Only two are named for want of payload space; the COUNT is exact and says `(+N more)` | 4 tags read on the new build + 3 compared candidate-by-candidate with `lf hid reader` | ✓ the count is checked against an independent tool's enumeration on every tag, not merely observed to be non-zero | ✓ the harness stayed green across the change | L249 |

Each was verified when it landed; none had been checked against the others. On `5a5fdf7c`, each protocol wiped, cloned by the Proxmark and read 4 times: **HID Prox, Indala26, Keri, NexWatch, Gallagher, Securakey, Noralsy, GProxII, AWID, Paradox, Pyramid, FDX-B — 4 of 4 every one.** ⭐ **And the rate did not move where it plausibly could have**: C287 added 31 `unpack()` calls per successful HID read, so HID was re-measured credential-scored at **24 of 24 exact, 0 wrong, 0 null** — matching C248's 48/48 on the same tag before any of today's changes. ⇒ The four stricter gates cost nothing on real tags, which is the claim each of them needed and none of them could make alone. ⚠ This is a REGRESSION pass, not new evidence for any single gate — it re-runs known-answer arms and would not detect a gate that is too LOOSE | 12 protocols × 4 reads + 24 credential-scored HID reads | ✓ every credential is one the Proxmark wrote from arguments we chose, so a stale tag cannot pass | ✓ the one change with a plausible cost was measured against its own pre-change number rather than assumed harmless | L250 |

New arm in `ctest/ambig.c`: for every format at every length, pack a credential and require the API's count to equal **this file's own per-format enumeration minus the excluded one**. Two different walks over `formats[]`, which is the whole point — the arm does not call the function it is checking. ⭐ **Sensitivity proven by deliberate break**: removing the `except` skip inside `wiegand_other_matches()` moved it from **0 disagreements to 1,519 of 1,519** and failed `make check`; restored, clean. ⇒ A table edit that changes what an operator is told now fails the harness instead of changing the message quietly | 1,519 packed frames across every format and length, before and after a deliberate break | ✓ the cross-check is an independent enumeration, not a restatement of the API — a bug in the API cannot hide in the expectation | ✓ the break was rebuilt from scratch (`rm -f ambig`) after the false-pass bug this harness was bitten by twice | L252 |
| C291 | ⭐⭐⭐ **THE FLIPPER FAMILY DOUBLES ITS REPEAT COUNT FOR PSK1 AND ONLY FOR PSK1 — two independent projects reached the same conclusion about the same family, from opposite ends.** U14, operator-requested. Audited all six reference trees on disk. **Three distinct models:** the **Proxmark** validates STRUCTURALLY and not at all by repetition — one capture, and it prints EVERY matching format with its parity verdict, `-@` merely repeating and printing each result independently; the **Flipper family** validates ONLY by repetition — N consecutive reads with **byte-identical** decoded data, any change resetting the count — and reports one protocol with no alternatives; **we** do both, plus C287's alternatives. ⭐ **All four Flipper worker files differ from each other and the agreement rule is identical in every one.** ⭐⭐ **`validate_count` is 3 almost everywhere and 6 for exactly six protocols: `indala26`, `indala224`, `keri`, `nexwatch`, `idteck` — the ENTIRE PSK1 family — plus `hid_generic`.** ⇒ This branch found PSK1 fragile from the other end: C190 measured our PSK1 emulation read **3 of 21** by a Proxmark where ASK read 16/16, because only absolute-phase encoding pays for a free-running clock. The Flipper authors emulate nothing and hard-coded twice the repeats on the READ side for the same five. ⭐ And `hid_generic` — the only non-PSK at 6 — is the format-agnostic HID reader, which is C284/C285 restated: when the layout cannot be validated, buy confidence with repetition. ⚠ **Official has no `indala224`; all three forks add it AND give it 6**, so the rule was applied deliberately rather than inherited. ⚠ **Why 3 and 6 is NOT established** — no tree comments it and none carries a test for it, where our own `INDALA_AGREE_COUNT` has a 50,000-trial bootstrap behind it | 6 trees, 24 protocols, 4 worker loops read | ✓ every claim cites the file and line that implements it, not the project's documentation | ✓ the four worker loops were compared by md5 first, so "identical rule" is a reading of four different files rather than an assumption of shared code | L253 |
