Ran the current shipping decoder over both committed corpora: **front, the documented placement — 160 captures, 110 frames, 0 WRONG**; **phasebits — 160 captures, 64 frames, 51 true, 13 wrong, and all 13 are DISTINCT**. ⇒ Agreement at 2 removes every wrong frame present, and 3 or 6 would remove nothing further because nothing repeats. ⛔ **And the note above `INDALA_AGREE_COUNT` is now history**: it warns that *"3 of the 21 front-side wrong frames REPEAT WITHIN A PHASE, two of them on 5 captures out of 5"*, which was measured before the straddle gate (C48) and Indala's zero-bit gate (C257). **Those 21 are now 0 on the same 160 captures.** ⇒ The paragraph is labelled as history rather than deleted — the 60-92 dead band is still out of `PHASE_ROTATION`, and widening that rotation would make the warning live again. ⚠ **This is 320 captures of ONE tag on ONE unit**; it says our 2 is sufficient here, not that 6 is wrong for a Flipper reading tags we have never seen | 320 committed captures through the shipping decoder, both corpora | ✓ the front corpus is the control and it has no wrong frames left to count, which is itself the result | ✓ "all 13 distinct" is a uniqueness check over the actual frames, not an inference from their being few | L254 |
| C293 | ⛔ **C269'S MECHANISM WAS WRONG, AND OUR OWN SOURCE HAD THE RIGHT ONE WRITTEN DOWN SINCE C207.** U14 asked me to place our rule against the references', which meant reading it rather than describing it from memory. **`lf_sampled_read_phases` requires two CONSECUTIVE decodes at the SAME sample phase to be byte-identical, and resets the comparison when the phase changes** — the source says why: *"a word decoded at one sample phase does not corroborate one at another"*. ⇒ **Cross-phase recurrence cannot pass the rule**, so C269's explanation of C266's three survivors is void. ⭐ The real mechanism is in `gproxii_read`'s own comment: *"at a fixed drive the distortion is the same in both captures, so they agree on the same wrong answer"* — deterministic distortion, not coincidence, which is why that reader pins drive 7 instead of sweeping. ⇒ Our rule is **as strict as the Flipper's and stricter in one respect**: theirs needs N consecutive identical reads, ours needs 2 consecutive identical reads *at the same sample phase* (C291). ⛔ **And a second stale warning found in the same file**, the same shape as C292's: `gproxii_read` still said *"this command ships as research, and the grid must say NOT VERIFIED rather than a read count"* — the open question it waits on was closed by C211/C213's 50ms inter-capture gap, after which the arm read 12 of 12 exact and 4 of 4 again today (C288). Dated, not deleted: everything above it still holds | the agreement loop and 2 protocol comments read against 3 claims | ✓ the correction comes from reading the implementation, which is what C269 should have done before inferring from an observation | ⚠ I closed this by saying **C266's 3 survivors are now UNEXPLAINED again**. ⭐ **C294 shows they are not**: the reader can only return a frame that matched its predecessor at the same phase, so those three recurred within a phase by construction | L255 |
| C294 | ⭐⭐ **C293 REOPENED C266'S SURVIVORS AS "UNEXPLAINED" AND THEY ARE NOT — the answer is ENTAILED by the rule, and it confirms C207 rather than leaving it likely.** `winner_res` is assigned in **exactly one place** in `lf_sampled_read_phases`, inside `if (have_prev && memcmp(prev_word, res.id, 8) == 0)`, and the function returns false when `ok` is unset. ⇒ **Every frame this reader has ever returned matched its immediate predecessor at the same sample phase.** So C266's 3 wrong credentials in 52 device reads are, by construction, wrong frames that recurred CONSECUTIVELY WITHIN A PHASE. No new measurement can add to that — it is what returning them means. ⇒ C207's account is not merely the likely one, it is the only one the code permits: deterministic distortion at a fixed configuration producing the same wrong answer twice in a row. ⛔ **And the reason my 48 broken-tag captures showed no within-phase recurrence is a methodological trap worth naming**: each was a separate `rdrcap.py` invocation with a 50ms gap and a re-initialised capture path, where the device's own loop takes its tries BACK TO BACK inside one read. Those are different timing regimes, and I compared them as though they were the same measurement. ⇒ A host capture loop does not stand in for the device's loop unless the timing matches | 1 assignment site and 1 return path read against 3 prior claims | ✓ entailment from the implementation, which is stronger than any number of captures and is what C269 and C293 both should have started from | ✓ checked that no early-accept path exists — with `tries` of 1, `have_prev` is never set and nothing is returned | L256 |
| C295 | ⭐⭐⭐ **THE WRITER NOW WARNS THAT THE TAG IT JUST MADE WILL NOT READ BACK AS ITSELF — 14 formats never do, and the reader's warning had no counterpart until now.** C284 measured the READ side on 29 real tags at one credential each; C287 made the reader name the alternatives. The write side said nothing, so `lf hid prox write -f IND26` produced a tag that reads back as H10301 with a different credential and the operator was never told — which C278 had already observed on hardware without my connecting it. ⭐ **Measured properly first, over a credential grid against the shipping `wiegand.c` rather than the single bench credential**: **14 formats NEVER read back as themselves** — IND26→H10301, INDASC27 and TECOM27→IND27, HPP32/KASTLE/KANTECH/WIE32→HCP32, N10002/SMP34/BQT34→H10306, H10304 and MDI37→H10302, HGEN37→P10004, **ACTPHID→C15001** — and **3 do so only sometimes** (OPTUS34 130 of 132, SIE36 65, P10004 33), which get a softer note. ⭐ **ACTPHID fills a gap C284 flagged**: the Proxmark refused to write it, so hardware could not reach it and the grid could. **Verified end to end on a real tag**: writing `-f IND26 --fc 1969 --cn 471` printed *"this tag will NOT read back as Indala 26-bit … reports it as HID H10301 26-bit"*, and the unpinned read then returned exactly **H10301 FC 123 / CN 4567** while `-f IND26` returned **1969 / 471** intact. ⇒ The prediction named the format AND was right. ⭐ Both lists are PINNED in `make check` | a credential grid per format + 3 device reads across one written tag | ✓ the warning's specific prediction was tested, not just its appearance — it names which format will be reported and that was checked | ✓ the pinned counts mean a table reorder fails the harness rather than silently making the warning wrong | L258 |
| C296 | ⭐⭐ **FIVE METHOD ENTRIES ADDED (M36-M40) — the session's transferable lessons were scattered across claims, where the next person would not find them.** `METHOD.md` is the file that holds method rather than results, and it stopped at M35 while today produced five lessons that generalise past this protocol and past this project. **M36 — entailment beats captures**: C269 inferred a mechanism from an observation, C293 read the code and voided it, C294 found one line had settled it all along; three claims to reach what reading one function gave immediately. **M37 — a host capture loop is not the device's loop unless the timing matches**: 48 separate invocations with a 50ms gap were compared against a back-to-back in-read loop as though they were one measurement. **M38 — an append-only document puts its oldest layer where the reader finishes**, and a superseded warning should be DATED rather than deleted because its reason is often still load-bearing. **M39 — re-running arms whose code cannot have changed proves the bench works, not the build.** **M40 — verify the FAILING branch of a display fix**: three messages shipped this session had only their happy path seen, and the one that was later reached turned out to drop two fields. ⇒ Each is written from a specific failure in this session with the claim that caught it, not as general advice | 5 entries, each traced to the claims that produced it | ✓ every entry names the concrete mistake rather than stating a principle — the project's existing M-entries are written that way and are useful because of it | ⚠ **method is the one thing here with no experimental test**; these are argued from single incidents and should be revised when one fails | L259 |
| C297 | ⛔⛔ **THE PROXMARK'S T5577 WRITE PATH HAS STOPPED WORKING IN THIS GEOMETRY AND REPORTS SUCCESS ANYWAY — the Chameleon's still works, so the bench is not blocked.** Restoring the bench credential after C297's write-warning tests, `lf hid clone -w H10301` printed `Done!` and the tag did not change; **three further attempts, each verified with `lf hid reader`, all failed** while the tag still read as the previous Optus34 credential on both tools. `lf t55xx write -b 0` likewise printed *"Writing page 0 block: 00"* and did not land, and **`lf t55xx detect` fails outright** — *"Could not detect modulation automatically"*. ⭐ **The tag is fine and so is the field**: `lf sniff` shows peak-to-peak 13908 with 3778 real field drops, all four devices enumerate, and both readers decode the tag's air perfectly. ⇒ **It is the DOWNLINK**: pm3 can hear the tag and cannot talk to it. That also retro-explains two things from earlier today that I logged and did not connect — `lf t55xx dump` returning all-ones and `lf t55xx read -b 2` returning an empty table, both of which need the same downlink. ⭐⭐ **The bench recovered with OUR writer**: `lf hid prox write -f H10301 --fc 123 --cn 4567` landed first time, read 4 of 4 by us and confirmed by the Proxmark as `parity ( ok )`. ⛔ **And the methodological point is that I trusted `Done!` three times**: pm3's clone reports success without reading back, exactly the shape M33 warns about. ⇒ Verify a write with an independent read, always — the tool's own word is not evidence | 4 failed pm3 writes + 1 successful Chameleon write, each checked by an independent reader | ✓ the field measurement separates "tag gone" from "cannot write to tag", which is the fork that matters | ✓ our writer succeeding on the same tag in the same minute is the control that makes this pm3's problem rather than the bench's | L260 |
| C298 | ⭐⭐ **THE OBVIOUS EXPLANATION FOR C297 IS WRONG — it is not a password — and testing it documented something nobody had written down: OUR WRITERS SET ONE.** C297 blamed pm3's downlink and left the cause open. The first hypothesis anyone would reach for is a T5577 password, and it had a compelling timeline: `write_t55xx()` calls **`try_reset_t55xx_passwd()` and then writes USING that password, default `51 24 36 48`** (`lf_reader_main.c:346`), and pm3 wrote fine all day until C295's tests, **which were the first use of our own writer today**. ⛔ **Tested directly and refuted**: `lf t55xx write -b 2 -d AAAAAAAA --pwd 51243648` printed its write line and **the air did not change** — still H10301 FC 123 / CN 4567, where writing block 2 would certainly have destroyed that credential. `lf t55xx detect` also fails identically with and without the password. ⇒ pm3 cannot write **with or without** it, so C297's downlink account stands and this candidate is eliminated by measurement rather than argument. ⭐ **The side finding is worth more than the refutation**: every write this project performs sets the card's password to `51243648` unless told otherwise, and that was undocumented. Anyone bringing a third tool to these tags needs to know. ⚠ Whether the PWD bit in block 0 is actually set — password *stored* is not password *enforced* — is NOT established, because reading block 0 needs the downlink that is broken | 1 password-authenticated write + 2 detects + 2 air reads | ✓ the test is destructive by design: block 2 would have wrecked the credential, so "air unchanged" cannot be a false negative | ✓ the tag was verified intact afterwards and needed no restore | L261 |
| C299 | ⭐⭐ **FIVE CAUSES ELIMINATED FOR C297, AND THE REMAINING ONE IS CHEAP TO TEST BY HAND — unplug the second Chameleon.** pm3 cannot write the T5577 while everything about both ends measures healthy. Eliminated, each by direct test: **a password** (a write authenticated with our writer's own `51243648` left the air unchanged — C298); **the downlink mode** (all four of pm3's `--r0`/`--r1`/`--r2`/`--r3` swept on a destructive block-2 write, **none landed**); **pm3's antenna** (`hw tune` reports **21.05 V at 125 kHz**, 15.93 V at 134.83 and *"LF antenna ok"*); **the tag** (both readers decode its air perfectly); and **the field** (`lf sniff` peak-to-peak 13908, 3778 real field drops). ⇒ **What remains is a hypothesis, not a measurement, and is labelled so**: writing a T5577 requires the reader to modulate the field with gaps the tag must detect, and a second antenna coupled to the same tag a centimetre away can smear those gaps while doing nothing to the tag's UPLINK — which is exactly the asymmetry observed, pm3 hearing perfectly and unable to talk. It also explains why OUR writer works: the Chameleon is the near antenna. ⛔ **The test is one action and needs no disassembly: power down Chameleon #2 and retry `lf hid clone`.** If it lands, the sandwich is the cause and nothing is wrong with either tool | 4 downlink modes + 1 password write + 1 antenna measurement + 2 air reads | ✓ every elimination is a direct test rather than an argument, and the block-2 writes are destructive by design so "no change" cannot be a false negative | ⚠ the remaining account is UNTESTED — it fits every observation and that is not the same as evidence | L262 |
| C300 | ⭐⭐⭐ **THE 12 CHECK-LESS FORMATS CANNOT BE HARDENED — MEASURED THROUGH THE SHIPPING `unpack()`, NOT READ OFF THE SOURCE.** C285 diagnosed the relabelling by auditing the unpackers, and that diagnosis has an obvious repair: implement each format's parity and the first-match walk stops mislabelling. ⇒ **The repair is only available if the checks exist**, and reading source is how this project has been wrong before (C269), so this asks the code. Each format gets 4096 pseudo-random frames of its own bit length — the length **discovered** by sweeping 26..56, never hardcoded — and the acceptance rate is counted: *k* check bits admits ~2^-k, none admits everything. **Result: 12 formats accept 100.0% of random frames** — IND27, INDASC27, TECOM27, IND29, ADT31, HCP32, HPP32, KANTECH, WIE32, OPTUS34, SMP34, P10004 — **19 reject some, 11 are the unimplemented enum entries**. ⛔ **Also corrects C285's arithmetic: "13 of 32" is 12 of 31.** `unpack_nonlinear` is a shared helper called by `unpack_indasc27` and `unpack_tecom27`, not a table row — C285 named it as a helper and counted it as an unpacker in the same sentence. The table has 31 rows. ⭐ **The list, the relabel map and the conclusion are unchanged.** ⇒ So C287's enumerate-every-match reader and C295's write-side warning are not workarounds for a fix that was skipped: **they are the only correct behaviour available**, and that is now measured rather than argued | 42 formats × 31 lengths × 4096 frames = 5,332,992 `unpack()` calls | ⭐ **the null is printed and pinned, not assumed**: H10301 carries two parity bits and accepts **26%** (expected ~25%), so the sweep demonstrably reaches the checks — a null near 100% would have made every 100% row worthless | ✓✓ the 12 reproduce C285's source-read list **exactly, name for name**, by behaviour rather than by reading; and the Proxmark's own unpackers for all 12 contain exactly one `return false` — the LENGTH check, which our `formats[]` already performs — with `Unpack_P10004` carrying the identical `// unknown parity scheme` comment | L263 |
| C301 | ⭐⭐⭐ **EVERY READER GATE MEASURED IN BITS — and 4 of 11 formats have no gate at all.** The four gates added this branch were each verified by breaking ONE named bit on a real tag, which proves a gate is not a no-op and says nothing about its strength: a gate rejecting one broken bit while admitting 99% of everything else passes that test looking healthy. New `ctest/gates.c` feeds each format's `accept` hook, reached through the **exported shipping descriptor**, 200,000 uniformly random frames of its own length. Totals (preamble + gate): **NEXWATCH 44.0, INDALA64 35.0, KERI 33.0, IDTECK 32.0, INSTAFOB 32.0, SECURAKEY 29.0, FDXB ≥28.6, GALLAGHER 24.0, GPROXII ≥23.6, INDALA224 30.0, NORALSY 19.9**. ⛔ **A PREDICTION OF MINE WAS WRONG AND THE CODE WAS RIGHT**: I predicted GProxII's gate at 2 bits, counting only the parity C255 added and ignoring eighteen spacer bits and the format-length gate already in `gproxii_accept`; it measures ~25. ⭐⭐ **The decomposition is the null that matters** — a 25-bit gate and a hook that returns false unconditionally are indistinguishable from outside, and the second would break every read while looking like excellent protection. Satisfying only the structural bits makes GProxII climb to **7.05** bits residual (predicted ~7: length gate + parity) and FDX-B to **17.61** (predicted 16: its CRC16), so both hooks are demonstrably reachable. ⚠ **The gap this exposes: INSTAFOB and IDTECK have neither a gate nor `require_repeat`** — 32 preamble bits and nothing else. INDALA224 and KERI also have no gate but do require the frame to repeat | 11 formats × 200,000 frames = 2.2M `accept` calls, plus 400,000 structured | ⭐ predictions written into the source BEFORE the run and pinned: SECURAKEY 10 → **10.03**, INDALA64 2 → **1.99**, GALLAGHER 8 → **8.01**, NORALSY 8 → **7.94**, NEXWATCH 4 → **3.99** | ✓ each measured figure matches the constraint count read independently from the source — ten zero spacers, two zero bits, eighteen spacers plus a length field — so the sweep and the code agree on structure, not just on a number | L264 |
| C302 | ⭐⭐⭐ **`unpack()` FIXED IN PLACE — the walk now prefers a format that can VALIDATE, and reports its own ambiguity.** Operator direction 2026-09-15: integrate the correction, do not ship a second function beside it; how it upstreams is a separate question from what the correct fix is. ⭐⭐ **The table already knew.** Every row carries `fields.has_parity`, and C300's behavioural sweep settles that the flag is accurate: the 12 rows with `has_parity == 0` are **exactly** the 12 that accept 100% of random frames. It was correct, populated, and never consulted by the walk — so this is not new data, it is the function reading the column beside the one it was using. **Two passes: validating formats first**, check-less rows only if nothing validates, and `verified` records which happened. `wiegand_card_t` gains `matches` (every format of this length that accepted, 1 = unambiguous), `verified` and `others[2]`; **`wiegand_other_matches()` is DELETED** — the count now comes from the same walk that picked the winner, so the two cannot drift apart. ⇒ **Measured effect: formats that never read back as themselves 14 → 12.** ⭐ **KASTLE — C284's headline failure, a Kastle tag holding fc 1 / cn 1 reporting as Check Point card 8389632 — now reads back as itself ALWAYS**; HGEN37 went 0 → 120 of 132 and OPTUS34 0 → 130 of 132. ⛔ **Not a cure, and C300 says why**: where nothing validates there is nothing to prefer, so HPP32/KANTECH/WIE32 still fall to HCP32. IND26 still reports as H10301 because BOTH validate and table order decides. ⚠ **The harness was grading the wrong algorithm** — `ambig.c`'s read-back arm called its own `matches()`, which walks in TABLE order; pointed at the real unpinned `unpack()` it revealed both the 14 → 12 gain and that the CLI's write-side warning lists had gone stale (now 12 and 4) | 1,519 frames cross-checked; 132 credentials × 31 formats in the read-back sweep | ✓ `unpack()`'s own `matches` against `ambig.c`'s independent per-format enumeration: **1,519 frames, 0 disagree** | ✓✓ `has_parity` is a STATIC table column written upstream, and C300's random-frame sweep is a behavioural measurement; they agree on all 31 rows, which is why the flag can be trusted as the preference key. Firmware links clean (334,388 B text); `make check` green on all four arms | L265 |
| C303 | ⭐⭐⭐ **THE RESEARCH INSTRUMENTATION IS COMPILE-GATED, NOT STRIPPED — and the cost of keeping it is 1,088 bytes of flash.** §9b had said "strip all three" since it was written. Operator pushback 2026-09-15: stripping useful tooling is counter-intuitive, what is the industry-standard way? ⇒ **Conditional compilation, and the tree was already doing it** — `app_cmd.c` gates whole handlers AND their dispatch rows behind `#if defined(PROJECT_CHAMELEON_ULTRA)` for the Ultra/Lite split, and this branch had already added `#if !INDALA224_READER_TRUSTED`. One macro, `LF_RESEARCH_CMDS_ENABLED`, defined **0** in `data_cmd.h`, so a plain build IS the shipping build; `application/Makefile` sets it to 1 and **that one line is what an upstream PR deletes**, instead of a hunt through five files. ⭐ **Measured both ways rather than asserted: off = 333,300 text / 164,744 bss; on = 334,388 / 168,752** — 1,088 B flash and 4,008 B RAM — and the gated-ON image is the same size as the pre-gating one, so the gate is transparent when enabled. ⚠ **One of the four is not a separate command**: the GProxII failure-energy payload changes the WIRE SHAPE of a failure reply, so it is gated with `#else` calling `scan_gproxii()`, the plain sibling already in the tree — it must not ship on, or a host expecting an empty failure payload misreads four bytes of energy. ⇒ **Nothing is lost, which was the point**: the capture probe that cracked C211 and the energy payload C206 turned on both survive at zero cost to a shipping image | two full firmware builds per configuration + one `nm` symbol diff + one hardware capability query | ⭐ **the 4,008 B of RAM is attributed TO THE BYTE rather than quoted**: `out.0` (4,000) + `buf.1` (4) + `nsamp.2` (4), all static locals of the capture handler | ✓✓ that attribution **confirms an existing source comment** claiming the probe itself costs no RAM — `m_samples` (0x7000) is present identically in BOTH builds, so the 4 KB is the command's chunk staging buffer, a different thing. ✓ And the host needs no gating, verified on hardware: `GET_DEVICE_CAPABILITIES` makes #2 declare **195** command ids including 3037/3038/3060, and the host already handles a command a device does not implement | L266 |
| C304 | ⭐⭐⭐ **C302 AND C303 VERIFIED ON HARDWARE — AND THE VERIFICATION CAUGHT A MESSAGE MY OWN FIX HAD MADE FALSE.** Flashed #2 to HEAD (`v2.2.0-534-g3f40bc5`, was `513-g96c9d1c`) by the targeted procedure: exactly one `nordicDfu` device with #1 still in normal mode as the cross-check. **C302: wrote KASTLE fc 1 / cn 1 and read it back as `Kastle 32-bit, FC 1 CN 1` — 4 of 4.** On the previous build that same tag was C284's headline failure, reporting as HID Check Point card 8389632. The HID tag reads `H10301` and now names *"1 other layout also fits: Indala 26-bit"*. **C303: 195 command ids declared, 3037/3038/3060 all present**, so the Makefile flag preserves the tooling exactly. ⛔⛔ **THE BUG**: that Kastle read printed *"the capture was corrupted … the numbers below are fiction"* over numbers that were exactly right, four times. `FOREIGN_MEANS_CORRUPTION`'s premise — an HID format accepts every valid 32-bit frame, so a genuine foreign tag never lands here — held **only** because the old walk took the first row in TABLE order. C302's validating-first walk lets KASTLE (which has parity) beat HCP32 (which has none). ⚠ **The harness had the identical defect**: its per-length arm asked whether an HID format ACCEPTS, not who WINS. Pointed at the real walk, 32 bits flips from *0 NOT → non-HID can never win* to **121 NOT → a genuine foreign tag CAN win** — and its own worked example is `fmt 12, fc 1, cn 1`, the exact credential on the bench. ⇒ The three 32-bit formats now fall through to the existing honest *"the reader cannot tell"* branch; **26 and 37 bits are unchanged and keep the corruption message**, because there the verdict genuinely still holds | 4 reads of the Kastle tag + 4 of the restored HID tag + 1 `lf search` + 1 capability query | ✓ the bench was restored and re-verified: `H10301 FC 123 / CN 4567`, 4 of 4 on our reader | ✓✓ pm3 independently reports *"found 2 matching 26-bit formats"* for the HID frame, corroborating `matches = 2`; and the harness's per-length example named the same KASTLE credential the hardware failure did, from a different direction | L267 |
| C305 | ⛔⛔ **DISSOLVED BY C325 — the rule was real as a pattern and wrong as a cause. The writes that failed carried the WRONG PASSWORD (`51243648` vs the tag's `20206666`), which has nothing to do with config words.** (original text follows) ⚠⚠⚠ **A T5577 WRITE FROM THIS DEVICE LANDS IF AND ONLY IF THE CONFIG WORD IT WRITES ALREADY MATCHES THE TAG'S. MECHANISM OPEN.** **Lands, 5 of 5** — `lf hid prox write`, whose block 0 is `T5577_HIDPROX_CONFIG`, *the value already on the tag*: `KASTLE fc 1/cn 1`, then `H10301` at `123/4567`, `99/1234`, `55/777`, `123/4567`, every one confirmed by pm3. **Fails, 9 of 9 across four protocols** — GProxII ×3, AWID ×2, Keri, Indala ×2, each writing a DIFFERENT config word; after all nine pm3 still reads `H10301 FC 123 / CN 4567, parity ( ok )` **bit for bit**. ⛔ **Refuted by direct test, not by argument:** the tag, field and coupling (HID writes succeed *interleaved between* the failures); time-ordering (HID still writes afterwards); the password path (the same `51243648` pair, and `t55xx_write_data()` sends every block TWICE, with the password and without); a preceding read; **block count** — ⛔ my own wording here was WRONG and the correction makes the refutation STRONGER. I wrote *"Keri writes 3 blocks like HID"*; `HIDPROX_T55XX_BLOCK_COUNT` is **4**. Measured counts: **HID 4 → succeeds; GProxII 4 → fails; AWID 4 → fails; Keri 3 → fails; Indala 3 → fails.** Same count, opposite outcomes. ⇒ **HID and GProxII are identical in every respect this code exposes** — same function, same field handling, same password pair, same block COUNT and the same block ADDRESSES 0-3 — **and differ only in the 32-bit values written** (C307 tick); and **my own changes** (C302/C303 touch no write path). ⛔ **ONE EXPLANATION IS DEAD AND MY REFUTATION OF THE OTHER WAS TOO STRONG.** ⚠ *Block 0 locked, data blocks writable* — I called this refuted because the loop would still have written GProxII data into blocks 1-3. **That refutation is WITHDRAWN**: the FIRMWARE transmits those blocks regardless, but a T5577 that rejects a write to a locked block may ignore the rest of the field session, in which case nothing lands — exactly what is seen. ⇒ It is the LEADING candidate again, and it rests on two unverified sub-assumptions (that a rejected write aborts the session, and that a write of IDENTICAL content to a locked block is tolerated, which is what HID's write does). *The packer returning `blk_count == 0`* is genuinely dead — refuted because Keri and Indala both carry the `== 0` guard that returns `PAR_ERR`, and all three writers queried directly returned **`LF_TAG_OK` (0x40), never `PAR_ERR` (0x60)**, so the block counts are non-zero and the writes ARE transmitted. ⚠ **The raw single-block path is 0 of 11 and is NOT evidence here**: the tree already documents `lf_t55xx_write_block()` as roughly one-in-three, because it charges the tag from cold and sends once — so it was never a valid control and is excluded from the count above. ⭐⭐ **AND IT IS BLOCK 0's VALUE SPECIFICALLY, NOT THE FRAME — entailed by data already in hand (M36).** The five successful writes carried **five different credentials**, so blocks 1-3 held five different 32-bit payloads and every one landed. ⇒ **The data words are demonstrably not the gate**, and since HID and GProxII are otherwise identical down to the block addresses (L273), the only variable left is **block 0's value**. ⛔ That is also what makes the result self-contradictory under every simple mechanism: if block-0 writes were merely IGNORED, GProxII's blocks 1-3 would still have landed and pm3 would read garbage — it reads a perfect HID credential, so **nothing at all is written**. A differing block-0 value therefore appears to abort the whole session, while an identical one does not, and no T5577 behaviour known here does that. ⇒ **The only surviving difference is the VALUE of the config word relative to the tag's current one**, and no mechanism is known by which that could gate a whole write sequence. ⭐ It would unify with C297/C299: pm3's `lf hid clone` necessarily writes a config word, and pm3 cannot write this tag at all | 14 write attempts across 6 paths + 9 independent pm3 reads | ✓ every before/after state read by pm3, never by the writer's own read-back (M33); the successes changed the credential four separate times, so the path is provably live | ⛔ **the mechanism is a blank column and that IS the finding** — "iff the config matches" is a RULE FITTED TO 14 OBSERVATIONS, not a cause | L268, L269, L270 |
| C306 | ⭐⭐ **pm3's DOWNLINK IS BROKEN WHILE ITS LISTENING IS INTACT — a SECOND, INDEPENDENT SYMPTOM CLASS for C297/C299, and it is reads, not writes.** Everything that only LISTENS works: `lf search` decoded `H10301 FC 123 / CN 4567, parity ( ok )` on every one of ~9 attempts across the session. Everything that needs pm3 to TRANSMIT fails: `lf t55xx detect` returns *"Could not detect modulation automatically"*, and `lf t55xx read -b 0/1/2` returns **the same word for every block** — `7FFFFFFF` at default settings, `0EAAACAA` once the modulation is correctly set to `--FSK2A --rate 50`. ⇒ **Identical words across blocks cannot be real block data**: block 0 is a config word and block 1 is credential data, and the tag is simultaneously broadcasting a valid H10301, so those two blocks MUST differ. What pm3 is decoding is the tag's continuous broadcast, not a response to a read command — i.e. the read command never arrived. ⭐ **This is the asymmetry C299 predicted, now shown on the READ side too**: not a second instance of the write symptom but a different operation failing the same way, which is what distinguishes a transmit-path fault from a write-path bug. ⚠ **It does NOT explain C305** — our own device changes data blocks on this same tag whenever the config word is unchanged | 6 block reads at 2 client configs + ~9 `lf search` reads | ⭐ `lf search` is the null and it passes throughout: the tag, the field and pm3's RECEIVE path are all healthy, so nothing here is a dead bench | ✓ the internal check is the identical-across-blocks signature itself, which is self-refuting as data and needs no second instrument | L271 |
| C307 | ⭐ **FIVE WRITERS COULD REPORT SUCCESS HAVING WRITTEN NOTHING, AND THE RAW BLOCK WRITER HAD NO CLI COMMAND.** Audited all **16** `write_*_to_t55xx()` functions for the `blk_count == 0` guard: **11 have it, 5 do not** — `awid`, `paradox`, `pyramid`, `gproxii`, `fdxb`, which are *exactly* the family sharing `fsk2a_t55xx_blocks()`. A consistent omission from when they were added together, not a random one. ⇒ Without it a packer returning 0 writes **nothing** and still returns `STATUS_LF_TAG_OK`, because a T5577 never acknowledges — a silent no-op reported as success. All five now return `STATUS_PAR_ERR`, as every sibling does. ⛔⛔ **THIS IS NOT THE CAUSE OF C305 AND MUST NOT BE CITED AS A FIX FOR IT**: all three failing writers were queried directly and returned `LF_TAG_OK` (0x40), never `PAR_ERR` (0x60), so their block counts were already non-zero. C305 made me look; the hole was real and separate. ⭐ **Also added `lf t55xx write`** — the firmware handler and the host binding both existed with no CLI command, so probing a tag block by block needed inline Python. That is precisely what the C305 diagnosis at the bench requires. ⚠ Its help states the unreliability up front rather than burying it: the raw path writes from cold and sends once, is documented at roughly one in three, and measured **0 of 11 here** | 16 writers audited; firmware rebuilt (+32 B text); CLI exercised on hardware; #2 reflashed and the HID arm re-verified | ✓ the probe write left the tag unchanged, exactly as C305 predicts, so the command is exercised without disturbing the bench | ✓ the audit covered every writer in the file rather than the ones I suspected, which is what turned a hunch into a family-shaped omission | L272 |
| C308 | ⭐⭐ **THIS FIRMWARE CAN NOW TRANSMIT A COMMAND INTO A LIVE CAPTURE, AND THE T5577 READ DEMONSTRABLY REACHES THE TAG — BUT THE BLOCK IS NOT DECODED YET.** C305 narrowed a write failure to one unobservable variable, block 0's value; pm3 cannot read it either (C306) and this tree had no block read at all. ⛔ **Two calls cannot work**: a T5577 regular-read answers only while the field it was addressed on stays up, and `capture_begin()` raises the field itself, so sending the command and then capturing resets the tag in between. ⇒ Added `raw_read_samples_probe()`, which fires a callback with the field up and settled and the ring already collecting, plus `lf_t55xx_read_block_probe()` and command **3063** (behind `LF_RESEARCH_CMDS_ENABLED`), chunked exactly like 3060 so existing host tooling parses it. ✓✓ **THE MECHANISM WORKS AND THE CONTROL PROVES IT.** The plain capture demodulates to a perfect 96-bit periodic frame — **self-agreement 1.000 at period 96**, the HID credential the tag is broadcasting. After the read command that frame is **gone (0.512)**, and the downlink itself is visible in the capture as field gaps at samples ~304-880 which the control does not contain. ⇒ The command reaches the tag and changes what it transmits, which is what entering regular-read mode looks like. ⛔⛔ **AND THE BLOCK IS STILL NOT RECOVERED.** No window shows 32-bit periodicity: p32 = 0.49-0.55 across four start offsets, i.e. chance. Bit timing is right (51.2 samples/bit against the control's 53.5, both RF/50), so the demodulator is finding bits — they just do not repeat at 32. ⚠ **Why is NOT established**: regular-read mode may not use the configured modulation, the settle may be wrong, or the tag may be in an error state rather than read mode. ⇒ The capability is real and committed; the measurement it was built for is NOT yet made | 2 captures of 8192 samples + 4 demodulation windows | ⭐⭐ **the control is what makes this reportable**: the SAME demodulator on the SAME tag recovers a perfect period-96 frame, so a failure to find period 32 is a fact about the signal and not about the tool | ⛔ **the block value is a blank column and that IS the finding** — the tool works, the answer is not in hand, and C305 stays open | L275 |
| C309 | ⭐⭐ **THE TAG NEVER ENTERS REGULAR-READ MODE — it keeps broadcasting its credential throughout, with or without a password. C308's open question is closed, negatively.** Switched from demodulating to **raw autocorrelation**, which assumes no modulation at all and so cannot be fooled by picking the wrong one. A 32-bit block at RF/50 must repeat every **1600** samples; the 96-bit HID frame repeats every **4800**. Measured on the 14336-sample captures: unauthenticated read **lag1600 = 0.440, lag4800 = 0.528**; authenticated read (`--pwd 51243648`, the key our own writers use) **lag1600 = 0.418, lag4800 = 0.510**. ⇒ **The 96-bit frame is present in both and the 32-bit block is in neither.** The tag is still transmitting the HID credential it was transmitting before the command. ⛔ **So the earlier signal was misread**: C308 saw the demodulator's period-96 agreement collapse after the read command and reported the tag's output as changed. Autocorrelation says it did not — the collapse is the downlink's own field gaps wrecking a short demodulation window, not the tag switching modes. ⚠ **What is NOT established**: whether the command is malformed, or is transmitted correctly and ignored. ⭐ But it joins a pattern — data-block writes are acted on, while block-0 writes (C305) and now reads are not | 2 captures of 14336 samples, authenticated and not, plus the 8192 control | ⭐⭐ **the method is the null**: autocorrelation needs no assumption about modulation, and it finds the frame that IS there (4800) while finding nothing at 1600 — a negative that cannot be blamed on choosing the wrong demodulator | ⛔ **I also caught my own statistic lying**: "best period" scored 1.000 at periods near the segment length, where only one or two comparisons exist. Re-run requiring ≥40 comparisons, those vanish | L276 |
| C310 | ⭐⭐⭐ **THE READ COMMAND IS WELL-FORMED AND THE TAG IGNORES IT — C309's open question answered, from air we already had.** C309 could not say whether the command was malformed or correctly sent and ignored. Decoding OUR OWN DOWNLINK out of the capture settles it. The firmware encodes a bit as an ON period then a field gap — `gap_sep_one` 54 Tc, `gap_sep_zero` 24 Tc, `write_gap` 9 Tc, `start_gap` 30 Tc — so a block-0 direct read must be a start gap then six bits **1,0,0,0,0,0** (opcode `10`, lock `0`, address `000`). ⇒ Measured on air by relative-dip detection: a 35-sample start gap at 303, then gaps at 356/381/406/431/456/482 with start-to-start spacings **53, 25, 25, 25, 25, 26** — i.e. **ONE, ZERO, ZERO, ZERO, ZERO, ZERO**, exactly the frame intended, with the one/zero ratio near 2:1 and no ambiguity between them. ⛔ **So the transmitter is not at fault and the tag is not answering a question it never heard — it heard a correct question and did nothing.** ⭐ The 8-sample-spaced dip train from 647 is the tag's own RF/8 FSK2a continuing, which is C309's result seen a second way. ⚠ **Absolute ON times are NOT claimed**: the detector reports ~17 samples where 24 was programmed, but an LC that rings ~400 µs cannot resolve a 72 µs gap edge, so the dip is wider than the true field-off and eats into the ON period. **The RELATIVE pattern is what this measures, and it is unambiguous.** ⇒ Joins C305 into one shape: **this tag acts on data-block writes, and ignores block-0 writes and reads** | 1 capture, 18 dips resolved, 7 of them the command | ⭐ the frame decoded off the air matches the frame the source says it builds — two independent derivations of the same six bits | ⛔ the absolute timing column is deliberately blank: the instrument cannot resolve it | L277 |

That only works if both halves are measured identically, which no improvised session will manage. `benchab.sh <label>` runs one battery and appends a comparable block: device count, pm3 `lf search` (listening), pm3 `detect` + two block reads (**the C306 signature is detect failing while every block returns the SAME word**), our reader ×4, a DATA-block write verified by pm3, a BLOCK-0 write verified by pm3, and a restore to the bench credential. ⇒ **Baseline `before` captured with the sandwich still assembled**: pm3 listening fine (`H10301 parity ( ok )`), detect fails, blocks 0 and 1 **both return `0EAAACAA`**, reader 4 of 4, data-block write lands (`FC 77 / CN 2468`), block-0 write fails, restored to `FC 123 / CN 4567`. ⛔ **The run order is the finding, not the script**: `nochamp2` — power down Chameleon #2 and change NOTHING else — must run BEFORE the sandwich is opened, because that single action is the whole C299 test and opening the stack destroys it irrecoverably | 1 full battery, 6 sub-tests, every tag state read by pm3 | ⭐ the battery is self-verifying: it ends by asserting the restore, so a run that leaves the bench wrong says so | ✓ the baseline reproduces C305, C306 and the 4-of-4 reader independently, in one pass — three standing claims re-confirmed by a script written after them | L278 |

the sampled readers and the capture-probe command, and the device dispatches **one command at a time**. ⚠ **This is an ANALYSIS of who touches what, not a measurement of concurrency** — labelled so deliberately. ⇒ **Sharing one buffer would return 28,672 bytes**, which is more than PR 1's entire RAM cost and would turn C316's likely-decline into a non-issue. ⛔ **Placement matters and the obvious
